Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

### **MODULE 23 - Technical Writing** 

###### **23.1 What Technical Writing Means in Research** 

###### **A. Concept Explanation** 

In a research context, **technical writing** mainly means **publishing articles in journals** . It is very different from other kinds of writing, and awareness of that difference is the key to doing it well. A recommended reference book is _The Craft of Scientific Writing_ (Springer), which treats a broader range of scientific documents, including articles for a general audience. 

###### **B. Step-by-Step: The Journal Publication Process** 

- **1** The researcher runs experiments or simulations and obtains a **new result** (new relative to all the information already available). 

- **2** The researcher writes an article describing the experiment, the results, the **significance** of the results, and the **context** in which they must be understood. 

- **3** A journal that publishes in the **same area** is selected and the article is sent to it. 

- **4** The journal sends the article to a number of **independent experts** (peer reviewers) in that area. Publication does not happen merely because it was submitted. 

- **5** Reviewers assess whether the work is **new** , **significant** and worth publishing, and send recommendations: publish directly, or clarify specific points first. 

- **6** The author **responds** to the recommendations. 

- **7** If the reviewers are satisfied, they recommend publication. The article is published and, for journals with online versions, first appears online as an official paper. 

**Why journals matter.** Established journals exist for specific areas (mathematics, physics, materials science and sub-areas such as welding or nanomaterials). Published articles form the **formal body of knowledge** that other researchers can consult. Work that is never published is not formally available to others. 

**How newness is shown.** The author compares the new work with **previously published journal articles** of other researchers in the same area. If it compares favourably, it is added to the same body of information. 

**Measure of scientific contribution.** The number of journal articles and the **journals** in which they are published are **one measure** (not the only one) of contribution. Some journals set very high standards and are very hard to publish in, so a paper there is assumed to be of high quality. 

###### **23.2 Journal Articles versus Other Literature** 

People are very familiar with novels, newspapers and magazines, but these are written for different purposes and in different styles from a journal article. The greatest difficulty for students is **failing to recognise this difference** . 

|**Type**|**Intent / style**|**Review**|
|---|---|---|
|Novel (e.g. mystery,<br>suspense)|Keeps the reader engrossed to the end;**hides**<br>**specific details**and springs a surprise at the end|Not a technical expert<br>review|
|Newspaper article|Reports day-to-day events with**urgency**; key<br>details, concise expert opinions|May have publisher review|



Page 15 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

|**Type**|**Intent / style**|**Review**|
|---|---|---|
|Magazine article|Extends newspapers by giving**context**; written for<br>a specific kind of audience (for example interior<br>decoration or current events)|May have a publisher<br>review process, not<br>equivalent to technical<br>peer review|
|Journal / technical<br>article|Conveys new scientific work**efficiently**and<br>clearly, giving results**up front**|**Formally reviewed by**<br>**multiple experts**who<br>judge it to be new work|



###### **23.3 Why Do Technical Writing? Why Is It Difficult?** 

###### **A. Reasons to Write** 

- **Primary reason: to get formal credit for the work.** Later people say that a particular researcher was the first to do it. 

- To **inform** people about new, important results. 

- The information gets used for other purposes such as **public policy** . Example: a published study that convincingly shows one fuel is cleaner than another may influence government funding of fuels. What counts is what has actually been **published** , not personal opinion. 

- It helps the scientific community systematically **keep track of accomplishments** and **share information** . 

###### **B. Reasons It Is Difficult** 

- **1 Students are unaware of what a journal paper requires** and so write things in an inappropriate format or style. 

- **2 Familiar literature misleads.** Habits from novels, newspapers and magazines are carried over unconsciously, but those writings have different audience and purpose, so the journal article becomes poor and is returned. 

- **3 High expectations of the first draft.** Typically, the first draft of the first two or three papers is poorly written from a technical-writing viewpoint; repeated corrections feel frustrating because the student does not yet see what is wrong. After a few papers the drafts improve. 

###### **23.4 Purpose of a Technical Paper: Inform and Persuade, Efficiently** 

The single idea that explains everything in technical writing: the purpose is to **convey work to the reader efficiently** . 

|**Function**|**What it means**|**Where it appears**|
|---|---|---|
|Inform|Present the data collected|Results|
|Persuade|Argue what the data mean (e.g. one fuel is cleaner<br>than another)|Discussion|
|Both must be<br>done|**Efficiently**, without wasting words|Throughout the paper|



Reading papers critically with these ideas in mind improves the reader's own writing; the first draft of the third or fourth paper becomes much better than that of the first or second. 

Page 16 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### **23.5 Parts of a Journal Paper** 

A paper has a **beginning** (title, abstract, introduction), a **middle** (details, results, discussion) and an **ending** (conclusions), followed by **references** . 

###### **A. Title** 

- Indicates the **area of research** and what is **unique or new** in the work. 

- Example: rather than only 'rechargeable batteries', a title naming a specific **mechanical failure of a particular rechargeable battery version** identifies both the area and what is new. 

###### **B. Abstract** 

- Appears immediately below the title and before the introduction. 

- **Gives away all the major findings.** This is the opposite of a story, where the main point is hidden until the end. 

- It is aimed at a reader who has **not read the rest of the paper** : avoid abbreviations that are defined later; use terms any new reader will understand. 

- The purpose is **not** to force readers to read the whole paper; it is to give the information efficiently. Readers wanting more detail will continue to read. Students' worry that giving away the best result makes the rest unread is unfounded. 

###### **C. Introduction** 

- **1** State **what the work is** (background). 

- **2** Explain **why it is important** (for example, interest in rechargeable power sources for portable applications). 

- **3** State **what is required to understand the work** , for example a model used, so that readers can look it up first. 

- **4** Cite **other published work** (researcher x did this, y did that, z did that) and point to the aspect they neglected, which this work addresses. This is where **newness is established** by comparison with literature. 

At the end of the paper, the **list of references** collects the articles cited in the introduction, experimental details and elsewhere, linking the work to previous work. 

###### **D. Middle of the Paper** 

- **Experimental details:** set-up, standard techniques and the parameter values used. Written clearly enough that another researcher can **reproduce** the experiment. Nothing is hidden, except material withheld because it is being patented, which must be clearly stated. 

- For **modelling** work, the corresponding items are the theory behind the model, how it was set up and how it was implemented. 

||**Result**|**Discussion**|
|---|---|---|
|Nature|A**factual statement of data**; no<br>judgment|Explains the**implication**of the result; an<br>argument|
|Example|'Hardness increases as grain size<br>decreases'; 'the curve shows a maximum'|Why the maximum occurs: two competing<br>parameters, one raising the value and the<br>other lowering it|
|Who can state<br>it|Anyone, even a technician operating the<br>instrument|Requires scientific background and<br>analysis|



Page 17 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

||**Result**|**Discussion**|
|---|---|---|
|Controversy|**None**: different people reading the same<br>graph reach the same statement|Different people may hold different views<br>(not arbitrary, but opinion based on|
|||analysis)|
|Freedom|Little|Much more freedom|



###### **E. Strategies for Organising the Middle** 

|**Strategy**|**Description**|**Example from the material**|
|---|---|---|
|Chronological|Present experiments in the order done:<br>first, second, third|Experiment 1, then 2, then 3|
|Spatial|Move through layers or locations|A nuclear reactor from the outer shell<br>(room temperature) inward to<br>thousands of degrees|
|Following a<br>variable|Track one quantity along the path|Viscosity of a fluid at different<br>locations of a flow|



###### **F. Ending of the Paper: Conclusions** 

|**Feature**|**Abstract**|**Conclusion**|
|---|---|---|
|Content|Summarises results|Summarises the major results|
|Assumed reader|Has**never seen**the work|**Has read**the paper|
|Terminology|Restrained; no abbreviations defined<br>later|More flexible|
|Future perspective|Not normally included|**May include**(e.g. effect of operating<br>condition on the better fuel)|
|Position|After the title, before the introduction|End of the main text|



###### **23.6 Language and Illustration Throughout the Paper** 

###### **A. Language** 

- Keep language **simple, clean, straightforward** . Avoid flowery language (found in novels and poetry). 

- **Avoid very long sentences.** Test: by the end of the sentence the reader should not have forgotten how it began or have to re-read it. 

- **Avoid phrases such as 'it is obvious'** : these are considered improper and arrogant. 

- The aim is that each sentence is understood in a single reading. 

###### **B. Illustration** 

- Choose among graphs, schematics, photographs, charts and micrographs the one that **best conveys** the idea; this is where creativity enters. 

- A well-designed **summary table** of many experiments gives readers a quick view of the range of parameters and the key points, and can be referred to repeatedly. 

- Match the **level of complexity** to the message: a schematic showing a satellite with two solar panels is enough if that is the only point; a photograph may distract with wiring, shades and reflective surfaces. 

Page 18 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

- A table of 16 (x, y) values does not immediately show a maximum; the same points plotted do. 

###### **EXAM TRAPS - Module 23** 

- Technical writing in research mainly means **journal articles** ; they are **peer reviewed** by independent experts (at least two or three). 

- **Primary reason** for technical writing: **formal credit** for the work. Number and quality of journals is **one** measure, **not the only** measure, of contribution. 

- Novelty is shown by **comparison with previously published journal articles** (in the introduction). 

- Abstract **gives away all the important results** ; novels hide them. The abstract is for readers who **have not read the paper** . 

- Order: **Title, Abstract, Introduction** (beginning); experimental details, results, discussion (middle); **conclusions** (end); references at the back. 

- **Result = factual, no controversy. Discussion = implication/argument, opinion based on science.** 

- Experimental details must allow **reproducibility** , except patent-related details, which must be indicated as withheld. 

- Conclusion may contain a **future perspective** ; the abstract does not need to. 

- Purpose of technical writing: **inform** (data) and **persuade** (discussion), both **efficiently** . 

- Avoid long sentences and phrases like **'it is obvious'** . 

- Main cause of difficulty: **familiarity with novels, newspapers and magazines** misleads students; also unrealistic expectations about the first draft.

### **MODULE 24 - Creativity in Research (Part 1)** 

###### **24.1 Outline of the Two-Part Study of Creativity** 

Creativity is often misunderstood. It is not limited to arts, movies or music: engineering and research also contain a great deal of creativity. The principles of creativity developed over the past centuries can make research more exciting and meaningful. The topics announced for the two parts are: 

- **1** Attributes of a fully professional researcher 

- **2** Sternberg's theory of creativity (Yale University) 

- **3** The 10,000-hour rule (Herbert Simon, Nobel Laureate) 

- **4** Role of stress in research 

- **5** Peter Medawar's (Nobel Laureate in Medicine, 1960) ideas on research problems 

- **6** Lessons from the lives of Nobel Laureates; traits of a creative engineer or researcher; ways to improve creativity (listed in the outline; **not developed in this part** ) 

###### **24.2 Depth and Breadth in a Researcher's Education** 

###### **A. Concept Explanation** 

Picture two axes: 'how much you know' against 'how many topics it covers'. At undergraduate level a person knows **a little about many things** . At the Master's level a person knows **reasonably well about many things** , and confidence in deep knowledge falls. At the Ph.D. level a person knows **a great deal about a very narrow area** . A fully professional researcher goes so deep in one narrow area that this depth gives a **vision** , which then radically improves the ability to understand other areas. 

The key idea: **breadth is gained through depth.** After a Ph.D. a researcher can sit in a presentation from a different branch (for example electrical engineering or metallurgy) and quickly grasp what is being said. 

###### **B. The Learning Curve** 

- A hot object cooling in cold surroundings follows an **exponentially decaying curve y = a e**<sup>**-bx**</sup> : initially the temperature difference is large so cooling is fast; as the driving difference falls, cooling slows and the object **asymptotically** approaches the ambient temperature (a first-order system). 

- Learning during a Ph.D. follows the **mirror image** : explosive learning initially, then it flattens. After about **6 to 8 years** the learning saturates, which signals it is time to move on. 

###### **24.3 Becoming a Fully Professional Researcher** 

A fully professional researcher has **something to say that peers want to listen to** . Claiming greatness is not enough. The following expectations apply: 

|**Expectation**|**Explanation**|
|---|---|
|Truly international|Not just local colleagues. Being invited to give a**keynote lecture**at an|
|peer group|international meeting; being one of the top 10 or 20 experts in a field.|



Page 20 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

|**Expectation**|**Explanation**|
|---|---|
|Command of the<br>subject|Takes years ('Rome was not built in a day'); achieved by long immersion.|
|Results that last|The Nobel Prize is**not given to upstarts**: committees wait**20-30 years**to see<br>whether concepts, theories and results have withstood the test of time. If<br>radically different results appear and contest the findings, the issue is still an<br>**open problem**. A long life is needed.|
|Mastery of techniques|Analytical, numerical**and**experimental techniques (example in thermal<br>sciences). Avoid dependence on one method or software, because it may<br>become obsolete (e.g. problems solvable analytically have decreased; flow over<br>a flat plate no longer excites interest). A diverse skill set avoids the**risk of**<br>**obsolescence**.|
|A line of investigation|After the Ph.D., settle on a line to pursue for the next**5 to 10 years**(30 years is<br>too long because much changes), where gaps and excitement exist and one's<br>skills allow a contribution.|
|Communication|Communicate through papers and presentations: poster, oral conference talks,<br>writing. Results alone are not enough; ideas must be accepted by others (the<br>Apple iPhone is quoted as an icon of creativity because it was accepted: no<br>keypad and no stylus when other phones had big keyboards, small screens and<br>styluses). Read widely, even outside one's subject, to see how different writers<br>communicate.|
|Guiding students|In academia, guide Ph.D. students sooner rather than later; be a**fountainhead**<br>**of ideas**. The successful researcher is not just brilliant in a subject but able to<br>**keep generating new ideas throughout life**.|
|In industry|Write and**defend proposals**for research and development to bosses, finance<br>and marketing; show your unique selling point (USP).|



###### **24.4 Attributes of a Good Researcher** 

|**Attribute**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|Sound grasp of<br>fundamentals|Basis built by B.Tech, M.Tech and courses; this is why research cannot<br>start right after school.|
|Mastery of techniques in<br>the field|A list of specific techniques to master (e.g. integral and similarity methods,<br>solving partial differential equations, finite difference method, measuring<br>temperature, pressure and flow rate in heat transfer).|
|Tenacity and perseverance|Experiments fail, programs do not converge, papers are rejected or wait<br>with the guide: stay resilient, positive, keep learning.|
|Scholarship|Deep knowledge of own subject**and**knowledge of what others (top<br>researchers, other groups and countries) are working on and publishing.<br>Avoid the**'frog in a well'**attitude. A Ph.D. problem becomes settled in 3<br>to 5 years, so leave the comfort zone; techniques learned still help.|
|Communication skills|Learn from lectures (NPTEL, open courses, YouTube) how different people<br>present ideas, even in unrelated fields such as law or psychology.|
|Creativity|**Often neglected.**|

###### **24.5 What Is Creativity?** 

A creative person **does things that have never been done before** . The first requirement is that the thing is new. The iPhone is highly creative because there was none before. 

|**Kind of creativity**|**Examples**|
|---|---|
|New knowledge in science and<br>medicine|Discovery of penicillin; the universal law of gravitation|
|New technology|Fluidised bed combustion (a bed of sand with high specific heat<br>in which many fuels, including biogas, can be burned)|
|Composing music|Mozart; A. R. Rahman|
|Analysing a situation in law,|A lawyer arguing a section of the Indian Penal Code in a way|
|philosophy or history in a new way|nobody has argued before|



###### **Law of Gravitation: F = G m1 m2 / r**<sup>**2**</sup> **(F is directly proportional to the product of the masses, inversely proportional to the square of the distance r, G is the constant)** 

Creativity is **not limited** to new mobile phones, computers or the internet; creativity occurs in many fields all the time. 

###### **24.6 Sternberg's Triarchic Theory of Intelligence** 

Many theories of creativity exist. The theory studied here is due to Sternberg of **Yale University** , who lists the 'must-have' qualities of a creative person. The source book is _Beyond IQ: A Triarchic Theory of Human Intelligence_ (Cambridge University Press, **1985** ). **Intelligence is divided into three types.** 

|**Type**|**Meaning**|**Where it appears**|**Nature of**<br>**answers**|
|---|---|---|---|
|**Analytic**<br>intelligence|Problem solving: obtaining the<br>correct solution when we know<br>there is**only one**solution<br>(academic)|Theory classes, labs,<br>examinations, grades, degree<br>certificate; everyone gets the<br>same paper, same time, no help;<br>teacher has the answer|Single correct<br>answer;<br>simulated<br>environment|
|**Synthetic**<br>intelligence|Ability to**combine existing**<br>**information in a new way**;<br>giving off-beat solutions|B.Tech/M.Tech project work,<br>design projects;**research is all**<br>**about synthetic intelligence**|Many different<br>solutions<br>possible|
|**Practical**<br>intelligence|(a) Adapting to everyday needs<br>with existing knowledge and<br>skills; (b)**selling one's ideas**to<br>funding agencies, managers,<br>editors and reviewers|Never taught; extremely<br>difficult to teach; acquired over<br>years|-|



**Education focuses on only one of the three.** Most education emphasises analytic intelligence, so synthetic and practical intelligence must be **self-learned** , and success depends heavily on them.

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### **Worked Example: Rating Problem versus Design Problem (Heat Exchanger)** 

||**Analysis / rating problem**|**Design problem**|
|---|---|---|
|Given|An existing heat exchanger|A required heat transfer, for example**100**<br>**kW**|
|Task|Find how much heat it can transfer|Choose the type, number of tubes, tube<br>length and tube diameter|
|Answers|**Everyone obtains the same answer**<br>(analytic)|**Different people can obtain different**<br>**solutions**(synthetic)|



###### **24.7 Knowledge as a Component of Creativity** 

**Knowledge gives the ability to recognise what is genuinely new** , and gives the skills to **design experiments** , **design new products** , **analyse experimental results** and **do scientific computation** . This is why research cannot begin right after school. Without knowing what has been done (through basic courses, advanced courses, literature survey, journals, reference books, textbooks) one may **reinvent the wheel** ; many 'brilliant' ideas have already been tried by others and failed. Some **humility** is required. 

|**Skill enabled by**<br>**knowledge**|**Illustration**|
|---|---|
|Designing experiments|A mercury-in-glass clinical thermometer cannot measure a liquid at**500**<br>**°C**; knowledge points to a**platinum resistance thermometer**, or an<br>**infrared**device for a surface.|
|Measuring flow rate and<br>pressure drop|A U-tube manometer reads pressure as**h ρ g**; if the pressure is large, the<br>required height h may be impractical, so other methods are needed.|
|Analysing results|A data logger produces a lot of data; analysis uses ideas such as**heat**<br>**transfer coefficient**,**Nusselt number**,**coefficient of performance**,<br>**efficiency**.|
|Scientific computing|Given a two-dimensional equation of the form ∂<sup>2</sup>φ/∂x<sup>2</sup>+ ∂<sup>2</sup>φ/∂y<sup>2</sup>= 0:<br>recognise it as a**partial differential equation, linear, elliptic**, with<br>boundary conditions on four sides (three kinds of boundary conditions<br>exist). Solution routes:**Fourier series, finite difference, finite volume,**<br>**finite element**.|

###### **EXAM TRAPS - Module 24** 

- **Creativity = doing things never done before.** It exists in science, technology, music, law, philosophy and history - not only in arts. 

- Sternberg (Yale): **Beyond IQ - triarchic theory - 1985 - Cambridge University Press** . Three types: **analytic, synthetic, practical** . 

- Analytic = **one correct answer** , tested in academic exams; synthetic = **combine existing information in a new way** , research and design; practical = adapt to everyday needs and **sell ideas** . 

- Practical intelligence is **the most difficult** and **never taught** ; education stresses **only analytic** . 

- Heat exchanger: **rating problem = one answer; design problem = many answers** (100 kW example). 

- Knowledge helps to **recognise what is genuinely new** ; without it one may **reinvent the wheel** . 

- Nobel committees wait about **20-30 years** to see that results stand the test of time; a long life is needed. 

- Master **analytical, numerical and experimental** techniques; a narrow skill set risks **obsolescence** . 

- A line of investigation spans about **5-10 years** ; a Ph.D. problem is settled in about **3-5 years** ; PhD learning saturates after about **6-8 years** . 

- Attributes of a good researcher (5 + creativity): **fundamentals, techniques, tenacity, scholarship, communication** ; creativity is **often neglected** . 

- **Breadth through depth** : deep specialisation improves the ability to understand other areas. 

- Exponentially decaying curve **y = a e**<sup>**-bx**</sup> (cooling of a hot body); learning follows the inverse pattern.

### **MODULE 25 - Creativity in Research (Part 2)** 

This module continues the list of qualities of creative people: **intelligence** and **knowledge** (Module 24) followed by **thinking styles, personality, motivation and environment** , together with hard work, stress, how creative ideas occur and how to choose a research problem. 

###### **25.1 Thinking Styles and Personality** 

- **Thinking styles:** creative people **question conventional wisdom, assumptions and rules** . 

- **Personality:** because of these thinking styles, creative people often get into **conflict with the society** around them. (This describes a characteristic; it is not advice to fight with others.) 

- To be creative one needs to be **persistent, tenacious, uncompromising and stubborn** : believe it will work and keep working at it. 

- 'Arrogant (?)' is listed with a question mark: there is a **thin dividing line between arrogance and self-confidence** . A small ('homeopathic') dose is acceptable, but not a large ('allopathic') dose. 

###### **25.2 Hard Work and the 10,000-Hour Rule** 

###### **A. Concept Explanation** 

It is commonly believed that innate talent or genius alone makes people great. The 10,000-hour rule says otherwise: **it takes approximately 10,000 hours of deliberate practice or study to master a skill or an area of research** . Anyone with above-average intelligence who puts in this time can become an expert. Examples quoted: Mozart, A. R. Rahman (began keyboard at age 3), Viswanathan Anand, Bill Gates; the Beatles and chess grandmasters are also cited. The rule 'debunks the so-called genius theory': hard work alone matters, which means **anyone can do it** . The question to ask is whether one has the tenacity to run the marathon. 

|**Hours of work or practice per**<br>**day**|**Period required**<br>**(years)**|**Comment**|
|---|---|---|
|3|10|e.g. practising tennis 3 h a day for about ten<br>years to become a school or district champion;<br>similar for karate, Bharatanatyam, violin|
|8 (full-time employment)|5|e.g. five years as Assistant Professor before<br>Associate Professor; five years for a Master's<br>degree|
|10 to 12 (about 60 h a week: 6<br>days a week, Sunday off)|3 to 4|Typical for a Ph.D. scholar, after which the<br>'vision' arrives and one is confident of the work|



Page 25 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### **B. Sources of the Rule** 

|**Person**|**Contribution**|
|---|---|
|Herbert Simon (Nobel Laureate)|Stated that it takes 10,000 hours of extensive training to excel in<br>anything|
|Anders Ericsson (Florida State<br>University)|Subsequent work; Chase and Ericsson (1982), chapter in<br>_Psychology of Learning and Motivation_, Volume 16, Academic<br>Press, New York|
|Malcolm Gladwell|Popularised the rule in_Outliers_(2008, Allen Lane), looking at<br>Mozart, the Beatles, Bill Gates and chess grandmasters|



###### **C. Hard Work, Failure and Frustration** 

- Science progresses through **hard work, failures and frustrations** . A first computer program will not compile, then runs but gives garbage; frustration peaks, then a break often brings the insight (a missing square root in a specific line). 

- Even highly accomplished researchers with hundreds of papers have faced rejection. They continue. 

- **Statistics of rejection:** if one reviewer rejects about 0.3 of papers, acceptance probability for that reviewer is 0.7. With three independent reviewers the joint acceptance probability is **0.7 x 0.7 x 0.7 = 0.343** , which is low. Rejection of some papers is therefore statistical. 

- If the work is **original, not copied, no plagiarism** , and the results are correct, try **another journal** ; the contribution is judged subjectively, and many journals are available. 

- Journals do not show the human effort (corrections, broken test tubes, failed programs) because society attaches too much importance to **success** . Biographies of scientists (for example Benjamin Franklin, Thomas Alva Edison) reveal the trials and failures. 

- **Dangerous correlation:** treating hard work as conditional on final success (no success means no hard work) is wrong; for example, low marks do not necessarily mean the student did not study. 

**Joint probability of acceptance by three reviewers = 0.7 x 0.7 x 0.7 = 0.343** 

###### **25.3 Motivation** 

Motivation **propels hard work** . Research students pass through phases of high, moderate and low motivation, so **self-motivation** is essential. 

|**Feature**|**Extrinsic motivation**|**Intrinsic motivation**|
|---|---|---|
|Source|External rewards: money, praise,<br>promotion, prizes, fame|Own goals set by the person; genuine<br>enjoyment of the work|
|Pattern|Work more if a reward is given|Set a goal, achieve it, then set new goals<br>and keep moving forward|
|Importance|Less important in highly creative<br>people; people often start with it|**More important**and harder;**very high**<br>**in highly creative people**|
|Characteristic<br>feeling|Linked to reward|The**'Eureka' feeling**from solving an<br>unknown riddle, unrelated to money,<br>degree or prize|



**Flow state:** when challenge and skill are balanced, a **sense of timelessness** appears; one is not conscious of body, food, sleep or the watch. This is the peak of intellectual ability and is 

Page 26 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### reached through intrinsic motivation. 

###### **25.4 Environment** 

- A **conducive environment is a genuine plus** but **not absolutely necessary** . 

- Example: **Srinivasa Ramanujan** (1887-1920, FRS 1918), born in Kumbakonam, worked under adverse conditions, without journal access or a big city, yet produced outstanding mathematics. Sometimes **adversity brings out the best** in a person. 

- Do not insist on ideal resources (a huge computer or perfect set-up). Seek alternative ways; that makes one more creative. 

###### **25.5 Role of Stress in Research** 

- The first step toward progress is **discontent with the present** . Progress is about **optimal disenchantment** (some unhappiness that drives improvement; too much unhappiness paralyses). 

- Achievement is about **optimal nervousness** . 

- **Eustress** ('good stress') is needed to bring out the best. Stress by itself is not stressful until it becomes **distress** . 

###### **Yerkes-Dodson Curve (the Human Function Curve)** 

Reported by Yerkes and Dodson in **1908** ( _Journal of Comparative Neurology and Psychology_ , Vol. 18) from studies on **rats** given electric shocks while doing an activity. Performance (y-axis) is plotted against pressure (x-axis). Human performance is taken to follow a similar curve (an **inverted-U** shape). 

|**Zone**|**Level of pressure**|**Performance**|
|---|---|---|
|Hypo-stress|Too little|**Low**|
|Eustress|Reasonable, optimal|**Highest**|
|Hyper-stress|Too much|Falls|
|Distress|Excessive|Very low (the study extends to death in<br>rats)|



The optimal eustress point differs between individuals (number of activities, sleep, leisure, research time, family time), so each person must **find his or her own eustress point** . 

###### **25.6 How Does Creativity Occur?** 

###### **A. Intuitive Flash of Insight** 

A new idea often arrives as an **intuitive flash of insight** , in which more or less the complete idea is revealed. It can come outside the lab (jogging, watching a film, in a bus). This vision is called **Darshana** . It does not mean the idea is proved. The idea must then be **noted down immediately** and worked on with hard work and logic; it may fail. 

###### **B. Worked Example: Planck and the Black Body Radiation Law (1901)** 

- **1** Earlier workers had proposed a form for the spectral black body distribution: Ibλ = C1 λ<sup>-5</sup> / e<sup>C2/λT</sup> . 

- **2** Planck found that putting **minus 1** in the denominator made the formula match experiment exactly. This was at first a **curve-fitting idea** (a flash of insight). 

Page 27 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

- **3** He was bothered by **why the minus 1 appears** . Working it out, he reached the idea that energy transfer takes place only in finite multiples of h ν: **E = n h ν** (n an integer). 

- **4** Hence **h is a fundamental constant of nature** and cannot tend to zero. This **marked the birth of quantum mechanics** . 

- **5** From the 1901 insight to the Nobel Prize in **1918** took **17 years** . Later work by Einstein and others followed (including E = m c<sup>2</sup> ). 

**Ibλ = C1 λ**<sup>**-5**</sup> **/ [ e**<sup>**C2/(λT)**</sup> **- 1 ] E = n h ν h = 6.627 x 10**<sup>**-34**</sup> **J s** 

_Note: the course content quotes h as 6.627 x 10_<sup>_-34_</sup> _J s; the commonly printed value is 6.626 x 10_<sup>_-34_</sup> _J s. If an exam option differs only in this last digit, pick the one matching the course content._ 

###### **C. Practical Lessons About Ideas** 

- Many brilliant ideas will come; the skill is deciding **which to pursue** . 

- **Time-box** each idea (for example work on it for two or three weeks, or one month); if it does not work, **discard** and move to another path. 

- Keep **backups** : a second and third line of defence, developed internally. 

###### **25.7 What Journal Publications Do Not Show** 

- A published paper (abstract, introduction, literature survey, methodology, results and discussion, conclusion, references) can lead to **misunderstanding of how science is actually accomplished** . 

- **Pains, frustrations and failures are not reported** and are not of interest to reviewers and editors. 

- This makes people **mistakenly think progress is uniform and linear** . 

###### **25.8 Typical Progress in Research: The Log-Sigmoidal Curve** 

###### **P = 1 / (1 + e**<sup>**-t**</sup> **) where P is the dimensionless progress and t is time** 

- **1 Initial phase:** very little progress while learning the field, preparing for qualifying exams, building the set-up or program. Be patient. 

- **2 Rapid phase:** suddenly the experiment or program works and gives good results. Capitalise on it and extract the maximum. 

- **3 Saturation phase:** further effort yields very little. 'Milking it dry' (writing a fourth, fifth, sixth paper on the same work) is not a good idea, since reviewers will not want more. 

###### **25.9 Principal Way Creativity Occurs: Alternative Views** 

Creativity often comes from looking at a phenomenon in **alternative ways** or by **rephrasing a question** . 

###### **A. Reductio ad Absurdum (Reduction to Absurdity): Proving that the square root of 2 is irrational** 

- **1 Assume** √2 is rational, i.e. √2 = p/q with p and q integers. 

- **2** Proceed with correct **logical steps** . 

- **3** This leads to an **absurd conclusion** that is known to be false. 

- **4** All steps were correct, so the **assumption** (√2 is rational) must be wrong. 

Page 28 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

- **5** Therefore √2 is **irrational** . This proof method was used by the ancient Greeks and is an exercise in logic. 

###### **B. Applying This Thinking in Research** 

- Look for **new performance metrics** instead of only traditional ones (for a solar collector, heat pipe or impinging jet). 

- **Do not restate the obvious.** Example: plotting Nusselt number against Reynolds number and concluding that Nu rises as Re rises is already well known. Be like 'man bites dog' (news), not 'dog bites man' (not news). 

- Example of a surprising and useful finding: as tube diameter reduces, pressure drop increases but heat transfer increases significantly, so the **ratio of heat transfer to pressure drop increases** , making a smaller diameter advantageous. 

- Results, discussion and conclusion should surprise readers and challenge accepted views. 

###### **25.10 Choosing the Right Research Problem** 

Joy from creativity is **intrinsic** : it comes from the sense of achievement and from finding something new ('from darkness to light'). 

|**Kind of question**|**Example**|**Verdict**|
|---|---|---|
|Trivial|Finding the inverse of a 3 x 3 matrix|Too easy;**not**a Ph.D.<br>problem|
|Extremely difficult /<br>high risk|Requiring about**50 man-years**of effort; a<br>thermodynamic proof that God does not exist;<br>a new theory of evolution|Too hard / high risk|
|In between, with a<br>worthwhile result|A question that can be worked on for**3 to 5**<br>**years**, giving a good answer and a couple of<br>publications|**The right Ph.D. problem**|



It is **surprisingly difficult** to find questions that lie between the two extremes and have a result worth knowing; for a professor or full-time scientist the challenge is to generate such questions continuously. 

###### **The Medawar Zone** 

Sir Peter Medawar (1915-1987; Nobel Prize in Medicine, **1960** ) described research as **'the art of the soluble'** . A plot of **payoff (return on investment)** against **difficulty** shows: 

|**Difficulty**|**Payoff**|
|---|---|
|Very low|Very low (trivial)|
|Very high|Very low (probably cannot be solved)|
|Intermediate (the grey region, the**Medawar zone**)|**Very high**|



The term 'Medawar zone' was coined by Craig Loehle ( _Bioscience_ , 40, 123-129). Each researcher must work out his or her own Medawar zone in his or her field. 

Page 29 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### **EXAM TRAPS - Module 25** 

- 10,000-hour rule: proposed by **Herbert Simon (Nobel Laureate)** ; work by **Ericsson (Florida State)** ; popularised in **Outliers (Gladwell, 2008)** . It concerns **deliberate practice** . 

- Hours table: **3 h/day = 10 years; 8 h/day = 5 years; 10-12 h/day = 3-4 years** . 

- Equating **lack of success with lack of hard work** is a **dangerous correlation** ; hard work is key, yet society over-credits success. 

- Joint acceptance by three reviewers each at 0.7 = **0.343** ; rejection can be statistical; if original and no plagiarism, **try another journal** . 

- **Intrinsic** motivation (own goals; Eureka feeling; flow) is **more important** than **extrinsic** (money, praise, promotion, prizes, fame) in highly creative people. 

- Conducive environment: **a plus but not necessary** . Ramanujan (1887-1920, FRS 1918) is the example. 

- **Eustress** = good stress; **distress** = bad. **Yerkes-Dodson: 1908, rats, electric shock** , inverted-U; performance is **highest at optimal stress** , low at hypo-stress and again low at hyper-stress and distress. 

- **Optimal disenchantment** drives progress; **optimal nervousness** drives achievement. 

- Insight ( **Darshana** ) must be **written down** and then worked on; time-box ideas; keep backups. 

- Planck: **1901** insight (minus 1), **E = n h ν** , **birth of quantum mechanics** , Nobel **1918** (17 years). h as quoted: **6.627 x 10**<sup>**-34**</sup> **J s** . 

- Progress is **log-sigmoidal, not linear** : **P = 1/(1 + e**<sup>**-t**</sup> **)** ; slow, rapid, then saturating. Do not 'milk it dry'. 

- **Reductio ad absurdum** : assume the opposite, reach an absurdity, hence the assumption is false (√2 irrational). 

- Right problem: **neither trivial nor impossible** ; about **3-5 years** ; **Medawar zone** (Nobel 1960, Medicine): maximum payoff at intermediate difficulty. 

Page 30 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25)

###### **2. Key Definitions (One-Liners)** 

|**Term**|**Definition**|
|---|---|
|Safety culture|Ongoing involvement in safety, not a one-time set of procedures|
|Safety glasses|Eye protection with full coverage that is reasonably shatter-proof; worn over<br>spectacles|
|Regulator|Fitting on a gas bottle's neck through which gas is drawn; makes the neck<br>delicate|
|Technical presentation|A repeatable, interactive, flexible event, not peer reviewed beforehand|
|Journal paper|A one-time, rigid, peer-reviewed, non-interactive, permanent record|
|Peer review|Examination of the work by independent experts for newness and<br>significance|
|Abstract|Summary below the title that gives away all major findings for a reader who<br>has not read the paper|
|Result|Factual statement of data, without judgment|
|Discussion|Argument about what the results imply|
|Creativity|Doing things never done before|
|Analytic intelligence|Getting the one correct solution (academic)|
|Synthetic intelligence|Combining existing information in a new way; off-beat solutions|
|Practical intelligence|Adapting to everyday needs and selling ideas to funders, managers, editors,<br>reviewers|
|Intrinsic motivation|Self-set goals and enjoyment of the work|
|Extrinsic motivation|Money, praise, promotion, prizes, fame|
|Eustress|Good, optimal stress that improves performance|
|Darshana|Intuitive flash in which the complete idea is revealed|
|Reductio ad absurdum|Proof by assuming the opposite and reaching an absurdity|
|Medawar zone|Intermediate difficulty of a research problem with the highest payoff|



Page 33 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) - NPTEL Study Guide - Part 1 (Lectures 21-25) 

###### **3. Master Comparison: Presentation vs Journal Paper vs Other Literature** 

|**Feature**|**Technical**<br>**presentation**|**Journal paper**|**Novel / newspaper /**<br>**magazine**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Nature|Event, repeatable|One-time record|General publication|
|Structure|Flexible|Rigid|Varies|
|Review|None beforehand|Formal expert peer review|Publisher review at most|
|Interaction|Very interactive|None|None|
|Results|Highlight as suited to<br>audience|Up front in the abstract|Novel hides key detail till the<br>end|



###### **4. Rapid-Fire 'Always / Never / Only When' Rules** 

- **Always** restrain a gas bottle with a chain at about 2/3-3/4 height, and especially once a regulator is fitted. 

- **Never** treat normal spectacles as safety glasses. 

- **Always** check that a glove is suitable for the solvent being used. 

- **Never** show a graph without axis quantities, values and units (and never a table without units). 

- **Always** include scale in a photograph or micrograph; **never** leave extraneous objects in a photograph. 

- **Never** read slides word for word; **never** flash slides quickly to fit a shorter slot. 

- **Always** give the key result in the abstract (never hide it); abstract terms must be understandable to someone who has not read the paper. 

- **Only** patent-related information may be withheld from experimental details, and the withholding must be stated. 

- A **result** must have **no controversy** ; a **discussion** may. 

- **Never** use 'it is obvious' in technical writing. 

- Innate talent alone is **not** what makes experts; deliberate practice (about 10,000 hours) does. 

- Conducive environment: **helpful, not necessary** . 

- Stress improves performance **only up to the optimal point** ; beyond it performance falls. 

- A research problem should be **neither trivial nor impossible** . 

**End of Part 1 (Modules 21-25).** Type **CONTINUE** for Part 2: Modules 26-30 (Creativity Part 3, Ethical Practices in Research, Intellectual Property Rights, Defining IPR, Design of Experiments).

###### **LECTURE 27** 

### **Ethical Practices in Research** 

Research ethics · Case study of fabrication · Historical milestones · Human & animal participants · Researcher obligations 

###### **27.1 The Research Process and Where Ethics Enters** 

**Concept in simple words.** Research is not one single act; it is a chain of stages. Every stage involves choices, and wherever there is a choice that can affect other people, honesty or harm, **ethics** becomes important. A usual (common-sense, not strictly scientific) account of the research process has **six steps** : 

- **1 Identify a problem or issue.** Researchers usually (not always) begin with a problem. Many problems are about resolving an issue in society that can sharply improve people's lives, so ethics already matters at this first step. 

- **2 Examine the problem and conduct a literature review** – study direct and indirect literature, i.e. review existing research in the domain to understand the problem from the right perspective and place it at the correct angle. 

- **3 Set objectives and form the hypothesis** – the problem and its expected outcome are stated. 

- **4 Decide the methodology** – how data will be collected (this varies from discipline to discipline). 

- **5 Analyse the data and obtain results/findings** – decide what kind of results are expected. 

- **6 Publish** the findings in a research journal – a very crucial part of the process. 

After publishing, one stage of research is over. A second stage begins when the research is **applied** in an actual situation (e.g. in industry). The application stage is outside this topic, but ethics plays an important role **from identifying the problem right up to publishing** . 

**Example.** At the publishing stage, typical ethical issues are **authorship** (whom to give credit when a paper is published in a scientific/technical journal) and **plagiarism** . 

###### **QUICK REVISION** 

- Six steps: problem → literature review → objectives & hypothesis → methodology (data collection) → analysis & findings → publishing. 

- Ethics matters at **every** step, from problem identification to publishing. 

- Publishing-stage ethical issues: **authorship** and **plagiarism** . 

- Research usually _begins with a problem_ (not always). 

###### **27.2 Meaning of Ethics and Why It Matters in Research** 

**Concept in simple words.** Ethics is about _good character_ . A researcher with good character is honest, fair and responsible. Because the public funds and trusts research, the researcher must be morally reliable. 

**Technical term.** The word **ethics** is derived from the **Greek word** **_ethos_ , meaning character** . Good character is linked to the **moral integrity** of a person; applied to research, the moral integrity of the researcher is extremely important. 

- Ethics ensures a **reliable process** with **trustworthy and valid findings** . 

- Research depends heavily on **public trust** ; hence researchers must be **responsible** for their work and are **indebted to the public** . 

- Many professional associations (in engineering, medicine and other domains) have their own **ethical codes and general ethical guidelines** relevant to their discipline, to ensure **integrity and public trust** . 

Page 4 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Ethics** ← Greek _ethos_ = **character** . 

- Two things ethics secures in research: **trustworthy findings** and **valid findings** . 

- Research depends on **public trust** . 

- Professional bodies publish their own **ethical codes** /guidelines. 

###### **27.3 Ethical Dimensions of Research** 

General guiding principles exist in research and ethics has a role in each of the areas below. 

|**Dimension**|**What it involves**|
|---|---|
|Selection of the research<br>problem|Ethics plays an important role right at the beginning, in choosing the problem.|
|Conduct of research &<br>relationships among researchers|Today research is highly**collaborative**: professional relationships,**sharing of**<br>**resources and laboratories**, and publishing findings must all be regulated by<br>strong ethical guidelines.|
|Treatment of participants|How to treat participants.**Animals**are involved in many scientific domains;<br>**human beings**are involved in medicine, biotechnology and (unavoidably) in<br>social sciences.|
|Truthfulness and transparency|Being truthful and transparent are basic, elementary ethical values.|
|Risk management|Research (especially scientific research) involves risk that cannot be avoided.<br>The aim is**not to avoid all risk**but to**manage it properly**: be aware of the<br>risks, be prepared to tackle them and intervene at the right time to minimise<br>them.|
|Willingness to share benefits|Every research has social and personal benefit (publications, patents,<br>intellectual property, other findings). Researchers must be ready to share with<br>stakeholders: fellow researchers, participants/subjects and those who helped.<br>**Sharing is not in the same way for all**– with collaborators it is_intellectual_<br>_sharing_(authorship issues); with research subjects it may have_financial and_<br>_other implications_.|
|Acting with responsibility|Ethics is essentially all about**responsibility**, which has a very wide meaning in<br>ethical research.|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- **Never** aim at 'avoiding all risk' – the correct attitude is **risk-aware, not risk-averse** (proper risk management). 

- • Benefit sharing differs by recipient: **collaborators → intellectual sharing/authorship** ; **subjects → financial and other implications** . 

- Ethics is described as being 'all about **responsibility** '. 

###### **27.4 Case Study: Fabrication of Stem-Cell Research (Hwang Woo-suk)** 

**Why a case study?** A real case shows how multiple ethical violations occur together and what the consequences are. 

###### **Step-by-step account** 

- **1** In **2004** , in an article in **Science** , South Korean scientist **Hwang Woo-suk** claimed to have **cloned a human embryo and extracted stem cells** from it. 

- **2** It was regarded as a remarkable accomplishment and he became South Korea's most prominent scientist, called a **'national treasure'** . 

Page 5 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

- **3 Claimed consequences:** an individual's skin cells could be used to make a cloned embryo, from which kidney, liver or heart stem cells could be extracted. These cells could replace diseased tissue with **no fear of organ rejection** (they come from one's own body) and could help cure diseases such as **Alzheimer's disease and diabetes** . 

- **4** An **academic panel** investigating his work found he had **faked** his claim: the claim of having developed **eleven patient-specific stem cell lines was false** . 

- **5** He admitted that **female researchers in his own lab had supplied eggs** for the research – an important unethical practice. 

- **6** The **2004 paper was based on fabricated data** to show that the stem cells matched the DNA of the provider, although they did not. 

- **7 Outcome:** in **March 2006** he was **fired from his professorship at Seoul National University** and was **charged with fraud and embezzlement** . 

|**Ethical issues specific to this case**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|Unethical and coercive behaviour|By the scientist and his team members (e.g. eggs supplied by junior<br>female researchers in his own lab).|
|**Fabrication of data**|The most prominent ethical violation in this case – data were made up.|
|Dishonesty / cheating|Basic honesty was violated.|
|Lack of trust|All basic principles of trust violated; public trust completely lost.|
|Lack of integrity|Moral integrity of the researcher was missing.|



###### **QUICK REVISION – HWANG CASE** 

- Claim: **2004, Science** – cloned human embryo, stem cells extracted. Claimed lines: **11** patient-specific stem-cell lines (false). 

- Most prominent violation: **fabrication of data** . 

- Consequence: fired from **Seoul National University in March 2006** ; charged with **fraud and embezzlement** . 

- Unethical sourcing: eggs supplied by **female researchers in his own lab** . 

###### **27.5 Associated Ethical Issues in Research** 

Beyond the specific case, the following issues are relevant to research ethics generally. 

|**Issue**|**Explanation (simple words → technical term)**|
|---|---|
|**Plagiarism**|Taking someone else's work and presenting it as your own – in writing,<br>presenting or publishing in journals –**without proper acknowledgement**. A<br>published article is presented as original work but is lifted from someone else's<br>work.|
|Unfounded charges against<br>another researcher|False accusations; often occurs in research laboratories and collaborative<br>research.|
|**Breach of confidentiality**|Participants/subjects (common in medicine and social sciences) trust the<br>researcher and reveal much information, believing the researcher will keep it<br>confidential. Privacy must be maintained; breaching it is a**very severe**<br>unethical practice.|
|**Discrimination**|Based on**gender, caste**(particularly in the Indian context),**race, nationality,**<br>**language**and other factors.|
|**Negligence**|Lack of responsibility; negligence can lead to harm. Researchers must conduct<br>research responsibly.|
|Truthful and responsible data<br>management|Data must be handled truthfully and responsibly.|



Page 6 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Issue**|**Explanation (simple words → technical term)**|
|---|---|
|Research involving human and|A very sensitive domain. First**avoid all harm wherever avoidable**; where|
|animal participants|harm cannot be avoided be prepared to manage it. Life is precious; show<br>utmost respect to human and animal life.|
|Social research issues|Several further issues exist in social research (not detailed here).|



- **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- **Plagiarism** = presenting someone else's work as your own **without proper acknowledgement** . 

- Discrimination bases listed: gender, **caste (Indian context)** , race, nationality, language. 

- Breach of confidentiality is described as a **very severe** unethical practice (subjects trusted the researcher). 

- Where harm to participants **can** be avoided it **must** be avoided; where it **cannot** , it must be properly managed. 

###### **27.6 What Research Ethics Studies** 

Research ethics deals with **norms of conduct** for researchers. Its main concerns are: 

- **Distinguishing acceptable from unacceptable behaviour** – e.g. plagiarism and discrimination are unacceptable because they are **impediments in the way of finding truth** in research. 

- Critically examining the **ethical questions researchers face** and how they ought to respond. 

- Understanding the **moral, cultural, social, legal and political implications** of research. 

- Basic **responsibilities and commitments** of researchers – to other researchers, students, professors, fellow researchers, society and the scholarly community. 

**Why implications differ.** The same research is understood differently in different cultures; research done in a distant corner of the globe can affect everyone (e.g. a pharmaceutical invention affects the health of people worldwide); **laws differ between countries** , so certain experiments cannot be conducted in certain countries while other countries allow them; and research has political implications. Researchers must be aware of and sensitive to all these. 

###### **27.7 Why Unethical Behaviour Happens** 

Chances of the research world encountering unethical practices are **greater today than about 50 years ago** . Reasons: 

|**Cause**|**Explanation**|
|---|---|
|Research is frustrating|Researchers may look for an**easy way out**, sometimes severely unethical.|
|Self-interest,**ignorance**and<br>**relativism**|_Ignorance_: not knowing certain practices are unethical._Relativism_: believing<br>something is acceptable when it may not be acceptable elsewhere or to<br>others. In**collaborative research**, all collaborators should agree on ethical<br>practices**before**starting.|
|Pressure to publish or obtain<br>grants/contracts|**'Publish or perish'**. Institutions appoint and fund researchers expecting<br>results; failure affects job security.|
|Career ambitions guided by<br>self-interest|Personal advancement overrides ethics.|
|Pursuit of**profit or fame**|Motivation other than truth/social benefit.|
|**Poor supervision**of students and<br>trainees; poor oversight of<br>researchers|Seniors have a responsibility to guide students/trainees properly.|
|Insensitivity to potential harm|Research consequences may harm human beings, animals or nature.|



Page 7 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Mantra for the pressure cause: **'publish or perish'** . 

- In collaborative research, agree on ethical practices **before** the process begins (to avoid relativism problems). 

- Unethical practice is more likely **today** than ~50 years ago. 

###### **27.8 Responsibility: Negative and Positive** 

Responsibility is the **key term** in research ethics, because ethical lapses can significantly harm other people. 

|**Type**|**Meaning**|**Details**|
|---|---|---|
|**Negative**<br>**responsibility**|Duty to**prevent harm**|Research work, findings, products and consequences (or even<br>the process itself) can harm people. Three types of harm to<br>avoid:**intentional, negligent and reckless**.|
|**Positive**<br>**responsibility**|Duty towards the<br>**public**|Work and career rest on**public trust**; the public supports<br>research expecting development/progress. Researchers are<br>indebted to the public and their work should**benefit the**<br>**public significantly**.|



###### **KEY ONE-LINERS** 

- Types of harm: **intentional, negligent, reckless** – all three must be prevented. 

- Negative responsibility = prevent harm. Positive responsibility = benefit the public (based on public trust). 

- **The single most important idea** in research ethics: **be responsible for what you do and take responsibility for what happens.** 

###### **27.9 Evolution of Modern Research Ethics** 

**Background.** Research is a comparatively modern phenomenon. People earlier also did research but in a different environment with different priorities; research as understood today came into existence from around the **eighteenth century** onwards. Early researchers were spirited people not constrained by the factors that constrain modern researchers. 

The **protection of human subjects** in research became a primary concern of modern research ethics, **primarily after the Second World War** , when the atrocities and cruelties committed by the Nazis against Jews, gypsies, old and sick people were exposed after the victory of the Allied forces. 

Page 8 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **Milestones (all directly related to medicine/health where human beings are involved):** 

|**Milestone**|**Year**|**Key facts**|
|---|---|---|
|**Nuremberg trials /**<br>**Nuremberg Code**|1949 (as stated<br>in the course<br>material)|Examined medical experiments on**concentration-camp prisoners**<br>**by Nazi physicians**. Toxic chemicals and other substances were<br>tested on bodies of Jews and gypsies;**euthanasia**was done on sick<br>and disabled civilians**without consent**. Showed the need for<br>**universal codes of conduct**for medical research. Ends and means<br>are intimately connected – a good end does not justify any means. The<br>Code gave explicit emphasis to**voluntary consent**.|
|**Tuskegee Syphilis**<br>**Study**|**1932–1972**<br>(about**40 years**)|Conducted by the**US Public Health Service**in the state of**Alabama**<br>on**Black people only**(racially motivated; no whites were studied).<br>Aim: to observe the effects of**untreated syphilis**. Participants were<br>told they were being treated for**'bad blood'**;**no consent**was taken,<br>the intention was not revealed. Treatment was**withheld even after**<br>**medicine became available**. Many died or were injured. Exposed by<br>the**media**; public outcry; study stopped.|
|**Declaration of**<br>**Helsinki**|**1964**|All biomedical research projects involving human subjects must<br>carefully**assess the risk of participation against the benefit**<br>(risk–benefit analysis; the study can proceed if benefits are greater).<br>**Respect the subject's privacy**(confidentiality, treat as a human<br>person) and**minimise the cost of participation**.|
|**Belmont Report**|**1979**|Issued by the**National Commission for the Protection of Human**<br>**Subjects of Biomedical and Behavioral Research**; another<br>hallmark in the evolution of research ethics.|
|**Animal Welfare Act**<br>(US)|—|Provides**guidelines and regulations for research with animals**;<br>researchers must value animal life. Many countries now have laws on<br>respecting animals.|



**Common message of all milestones (Nuremberg to Belmont):** the need for **voluntary consent** from participants, respect for human rights, confidentiality of information, and recognition of the **autonomy of the individual** (each person is an autonomous entity to be respected simply because he/she is a human being). 

**Institutional mechanism.** Research institutions and organisations now have **ethics committees or Institutional Review Boards (IRBs)** that examine research within the institution and suggest ethical guidelines to be followed. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Order in which the milestones are listed: **Nuremberg (1949) → Tuskegee (1932–1972, exposed by media in 1972) → Helsinki (1964) → Belmont (1979)** . 

- Tuskegee subjects: **Black people only (no whites)** ; told 'bad blood'; **no consent** ; **treatment withheld although available** ; run by **US Public Health Service** in **Alabama** . 

- Nuremberg Code → central emphasis: **voluntary consent** . Helsinki → **risk–benefit assessment** , privacy, minimise cost. 

- Belmont Report issuer: **National Commission for the Protection of Human Subjects of Biomedical and Behavioral Research** . 

- Animal research regulation: **Animal Welfare Act (US)** . Institutional oversight: **ethics committee / Institutional Review Board** . 

- Modern research ethics grew mainly from concern for **human subjects after the Second World War** . 

###### **27.10 Ethics for Human Participants** 

When human beings are research subjects, these principles apply: 

Page 9 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Principle**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|**Consent**|Participants agree to take part after being given**all available information**about the<br>study (informed, voluntary consent).|
|**Withdrawal**|Participants may**withdraw at any stage**of the study and/or**refuse to take part**.|
|**Confidentiality**|Personal or identifiable information must**not be disclosed without the participant's**<br>**consent**. When a study is published, withhold name, address and similar details that<br>could identify the person.|
|**Security**|Data and samples collected must be kept secure and**anonymised where appropriate**.|
|**Safety**|Participants must not be exposed to**unnecessary or disproportionate levels of risk**.|



###### **MEMORY AID – FIVE PRINCIPLES** 

- **C** onsent · **W** ithdrawal · **C** onfidentiality · **S** ecurity (anonymised) · **S** afety (no disproportionate risk). 

###### **27.11 Ethics for Animal Research** 

Animal life is also precious. **Uses of animals in research:** 

- As **models for humans** to understand disease processes (biomedical and pharmaceutical research). 

- To develop **preventive and therapeutic measures** such as vaccines or medicines. 

- In **toxicity testing** – exposing animals to toxic substances to study impact and cure. 

**Ethical questions raised** (all involve eventual harm to animals): 

|**Question**|**Discussion**|
|---|---|
|Do we have the right to harm<br>animals?|For adult humans**consent is taken**;**animals cannot give permission**.<br>Animals may be used**only when it becomes necessary**, not abruptly.|
|'We eat animals anyway – so why<br>not experiment?'|Is using animals for food at the same level as using them for<br>experimentation? Can they be compared? The argument is questioned.|
|Can all species be used?|Commonly used:**mice and rats**(pharmaceutical research);**monkeys**(close<br>to humans). Question: can**chimpanzees**be used, given evidence of<br>advanced brain skills? What criterion decides?|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Human subjects: **consent is taken** . Animals: **consent cannot be taken** – hence use only when **necessary** . 

- Animals used as **models** , for **vaccine/medicine development** and **toxicity testing** . 

- Commonly used: **mice, rats, monkeys** ; chimpanzees raise a particular ethical doubt (advanced brain). 

Page 10 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **27.12 Obligations of Researchers** 

|**Obligation**|**Explanation**|
|---|---|
|**Honesty**|To oneself, to the work, to society and to fellow researchers.|
|**Integrity**|Moral integrity throughout research.|
|**Minimal possible risk**|To participants**and to the researchers themselves**.|
|**Cultural sensitivity**|Be aware of cultural factors where research is conducted.|
|**Transparency**|**Before**research begins, the permission of the organisation's authorities – through<br>an**Institutional Review Board or ethics committee**– is**mandatory**. The<br>researcher must reveal information about the research process and**report**<br>**wherever a risk or harm is anticipated**.|
|**Readiness to share**<br>**benefits**|Credit is shared with fellow researchers/collaborators (e.g.**co-authors**). Issues:<br>who is first, second, third author; whether a collaborator can be named an author.<br>Recognise contributions**on merit**:**substantial contribution to the ideas in**<br>**the paper → authorship**;**only help here and there → acknowledgement**.<br>The objective of research is**social benefit, not name and fame**.|
|**Social benefit over**<br>**individual benefit**|Researchers have responsibilities to society; research rests on public trust.|
|**Proper risk management**|Be**risk aware, not risk averse**.|



###### **Other important issues in research ethics:** 

- **Social benefit** : some research has an explicit, direct positive impact, some does not; responsibilities of researchers and **funding agencies** must be considered. 

- Be **responsible mentors** to other researchers. 

- **Collaborative research** : publication and authorship issues. 

- **Respect intellectual property rights** of other researchers – this is how you contribute to the scholarly community (this leads to the next topic, IPR). 

- **Do not discriminate; be responsible and take responsibility.** 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Authorship rule: **substantial contribution to ideas → authorship; minor help → acknowledgement only** . 'Helped a couple of times' does **not** justify authorship. 

- Permission of IRB/ethics committee is **mandatory before** research begins (not after). 

- Social benefit is **more important than individual benefit** ; the objective of research is **not name and fame** . 

- Minimal risk applies to participants **and** the researchers themselves.
