###### **LECTURE 28** 

### **Intellectual Property Rights – An Introduction** 

Rights · Property · Intellectual · Definition · Characteristics · Non-rivalrous & non-excludable · Types of IPR 

###### **28.1 Roadmap: Breaking IPR into Three Words** 

To understand **Intellectual Property Rights (IPR)** , the term is broken into its independent ingredients: **Rights** , **Property** and **Intellectual** . Once each is understood, the common traits of IPR emerge, and a definition can be attempted. **There is no universal definition of IPR as of now.** After the definition come the **types** of IPR (a long and growing list), the **need** for IPR in today's world and its **relevance** in the modern world. 

###### **28.2 What Is Intellectual Property (IP)?** 

**Simple language.** Intellectual property is what a person creates with his or her mind – an idea, an invention, a book, a song – as opposed to a physical thing like a house or a pen. 

**Technical statement.** Intellectual property relates to **original ideas, results that emanate from research** , and generally covers some kind of **critical business information** . It is the set of property that emanates from **human creative labour** . 

**IP versus real property.** Real property (land, a laptop, a pen) is **tangible** : you can touch and feel it, it exists in **time and space** , it has borders, and there is no real dispute about its contours (a land is defined by its boundaries). For **intellectual property** there are issues in understanding the **outer limits, boundaries or private space** of the property. 

**IP versus IPR.** IP is the set of _creations of the human mind_ ; IPR is the _right that protects these manifestations_ . They are used **interchangeably** in most places (nothing wrong with that, since talking about IP usually also covers the corresponding rights), but for understanding, the ingredients of IPR are examined separately. 

|**Form in which IP manife**|**sts**|**Protected by which IPR**|
|---|---|---|
|An**invention**||**Patent**|
|A**literary work**(book / wo|rk expressed in words)|**Copyright**|
|**Feature**|**Real (tangible) property**|**Intellectual property**|
|Nature|Tangible – can be touched and felt|Intangible – creation of the mind|
|Exists in time and space?|**Yes**|**No**|
|Boundaries|Clear (land boundaries, measurable<br>extent)|Not obvious; must be**described**(e.g.<br>patent claims)|
|Examples|Land, laptop, pen|Invention, idea expression, literary work|



###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- IP = property emanating from **human creative labour** (original ideas, research results, critical business information). 

- IP = the creation; **IPR = the right protecting it** ; the terms are commonly used interchangeably. 

- **No universal definition** of IPR exists at present. 

Page 13 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **28.3 The 'Rights' Part** 

**Definition.** A **right** is a **legal entitlement** – something you are entitled to get legally. It can be **justified, recognised and protected** ; its **violation is deemed unlawful** and leaves the person whose right is violated with a **remedy** . 

###### **Two broad senses of 'right'** 

|**Sense**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|**Liberty**|Your ability or freedom to do something.|
|**License**|Your right to do something because somebody has given consent / allowed it.|



###### **Kinds of rights** 

|**Classification**|**Explanation**|**Examples**|
|---|---|---|
|**Inherent rights**|Manifest in a human being because he/she is a human<br>being.|Human rights: right to vote, right<br>to privacy.|
|**Rights on things**|Rights manifesting in things beyond the human being<br>(humans can possess, own and transfer things).<br>**Created by law**– legal recognition is needed for<br>something to be a right.|Property rights.|
|**General rights**|Shared by people; every citizen can claim and gets<br>them.|Protection a citizen expects from<br>the government.|
|**Exclusive rights**|Rights exercised**exclusively**; confer the**ability to**<br>**stop others from doing things without the**<br>**owner's consent**.|Owning a property → right to<br>exclude others from<br>entering/enjoying it; owning a book<br>→ right to exclude others from<br>reading/using it.|



###### **Rights in connection with IP** 

Rights emanating from IP are those **capable of being protected** . For an artistic work (a book), the creator/owner can stop others from using it, copying it or disseminating information from it, because of an **exclusive right** – the right is called **copyright** . If the exclusive right vests in an invention, it is called a **patent right** . 

###### **FIVE ACTS CONTROLLED BY A PATENT** 

###### **Patent right = right to make (manufacture) · sell · offer for sale · import · use the invention** 

**Infringement.** Doing any of these acts **without the right owner's consent** violates the owner's right. A violation of the right of an IPR owner is called **infringement** . Technically, **infringement means trespass** – entering another's property. Just as a 'trespassers will be prosecuted' notice on private property signals both **civil and criminal remedies** for trespass, trespass on intellectual property is called infringement. Any intrusion into any of the five acts vis-à-vis the invention is an infringement. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- **Infringement = trespass** on intellectual property. 

- Exclusive right = ability to **stop others** from acting **without consent** . 

- Inherent rights (e.g. right to vote, privacy) belong to a person as a human; property rights are **created by law** and need **legal recognition** . 

- A right always carries a **remedy** upon violation; its violation is **unlawful** . 

- A patent covers five acts: make, use, sell, offer for sale, **import** . 

Page 14 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **28.4 The 'Property' Part** 

**Simple language.** Property is anything that can be owned, distinguished from somebody else's, processed and transferred. Property is a form of **regulation** ; intellectual property is a form of regulation of **creations that come out of the mind** . 

**Two broad classifications: public property** (held in common – 'the commons') and **private property** (held by a person individually). 

- **Understanding property through the land analogy** 

- **1** Landed property exists in **time and space** ; you can go to the location and see its boundaries and its neighbours' boundaries. 

- **2** The hallmark of property is that it can be **distinguished from another's property** – this comes from its **limits (boundaries)** . 

- **3** Land can be **measured** and expressed in numbers (extent). 

- **4** Title is documented in a **deed (sale deed / title deed)** . Towards the end, the **schedule** describes the land, its extent and its boundaries. 

- **5** So there are **two ways to check boundaries** : physically inspect the land, or read the title deed. 

###### **Why IP is different** 

IP does **not exist in time and space** , so some way is needed to ascertain its limits. **Example (invention):** an improvement in an internal combustion engine rests _inside_ the engine; looking at the engine from outside one cannot find where the improvement lies unless it is **described in detail in writing** (or someone reverse-engineers it). The inventor must tell the world what the contribution is. 

|**Land (real property)**|**Patent (intellectual property)**|
|---|---|
|Title deed / sale deed|**Patent specification**(written description of the<br>invention)|
|**Schedule**at the end listing extent and boundaries|**Claims**at the end of the specification|
|Boundaries physically ascertainable|**Claims demarcate the boundaries**of the intellectual<br>property|
|Check by inspection or by deed|Check by reading the specification, mainly the claims|



**Key patent rule.** In patent law every invention must be **described in writing** in great detail, differentiating it from every other earlier invention close to it. What went before is called the **prior art** . After explaining the inventive contribution over the prior art, the inventor claims property in the **claims** . **Protection is confined to what is mentioned in the claims, not to what is mentioned in the descriptive part** of the specification. 

**Ownership disputes – pen example.** For a physical pen, disputes of ownership are settled by: (1) a **secret mark** (e.g. a name written discreetly), (2) **possession for a long uninterrupted period** , (3) **evidence of title** – a bill/receipt or proof of receiving it as a gift. Because intellectual property is not readily discernible and does not manifest in tangible form, such disputes are **difficult to settle** ; this is why patents demand a detailed written disclosure. 

**Is writing enough to get a patent?** No. The patent office scrutinises the written description (in India, the **Indian Patent Office** ). Only after passing the tests is a patent granted, giving **title and protection for a limited period of time, provided it is kept alive by paying the required fees** . 

Page 15 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Land ↔ patent analogy: **sale deed ↔ patent specification** ; **schedule ↔ claims** . 

- Scope of patent protection = the **claims** – **never** the descriptive part. 

- Prior art = what has gone before the invention. 

- Patents are granted for a **limited period** and only if **fees are paid** to keep them alive. 

- Property types: **public (commons)** vs **private** . 

- IP does **not** exist in time and space (unlike land or a pen). 

###### **28.5 The 'Intellectual' Part** 

**Simple language.** 'Intellectual' refers to things that come out of our **intellect** – our ability to think and understand ideas (even complicated ones). An idea is a **product of mental effort or careful thinking** . 

Intellectual covers **creativity inherent in human beings, creativity from intellectual effort, and ideas from constant/careful thinking** . IPR is _descriptive of the character of what it protects_ : e.g. patents (the IPR) protect inventions (the IP), which are products of creative human labour. Even a **serendipitous invention** is attributed to the intellect. 

|**Illustration**|**What is visible**|**What is intellectual**|
|---|---|---|
|**Carpenter making a**<br>**chair**|Physical labour (sawing, cutting,<br>aligning).|The**mental effort**of conceiving and putting the<br>chair together – not discernible while watching,<br>though it is there. Physical and mental skills<br>combine and are hard to separate.|
|**Author writing a**<br>**book**|Putting pen on paper for days – looks<br>the same for any author (textbook,<br>letter, masterpiece).|The mental/creative effort differs, and so does<br>the quality of what is produced.|
|**Tailor vs sewing**<br>**machine**|Embroidery on cloth with colourful<br>threads.|A tailor's embroidery involves intellectual effort;<br>the**machine's work is not called IP**– the<br>intellectual effort went into**creating the**<br>**machine**.|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- 'Intellectual' means something that comes from **human beings** – **not from machines** and not from other beings. 

- Intellectual effort = a combination of mental and physical skills, with the mental/creative element being special to humans. 

- A **serendipitous (accidental) invention** is still treated as a product of the intellect. 

###### **28.6 Defining Intellectual Property Rights** 

**One working definition:** Intellectual property rights generally protect **applications of ideas and information that are of commercial value** . 

**Crucial point:** IPR do **not protect ideas per se** . Ideas by themselves cannot be protected; the **applications and expressions** of ideas can. 

**Example – crime fiction.** The crime-fiction genre (a who-done-it or how-done-it plot with an event, suspicious characters, a solver and a twist) has existed for a long time and is predictable. Yet authors such as **Agatha Christie** wrote many books in the same genre, sometimes with the same main character; each book expresses the idea in a **different form** , so **copyright subsists separately in each book** . It is no objection that all are crime novels. **As long as the expression is different, separate rights subsist.** 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- IPR protect **applications/expressions** of ideas – **not ideas themselves** . 

- Genre or common idea ≠ infringement; **different expression → separate copyright** (Agatha Christie example). 

Page 16 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **28.7 Five Traits (Characteristics) of IPR** 

|**#**|**Trait**|**Explanation**|
|---|---|---|
|1|**Enforceable**(protectable by<br>law)|Must be in the nature of a right: an entitlement that can be claimed and, if<br>violated, gets a**remedy**– stopping the infringer or claiming**monetary**<br>**compensation (damages)**. Valuable information that the law does not<br>protect is not an IPR. Listed rights: patent, copyright, trade mark, design, etc.|
|2|**Intangible but**<br>**describable and**<br>**registrable**|Different from normal property; manifests in the end product but must be<br>**described**. Patent: written specification → patent**prosecution**in the patent<br>office → grant (title). Designs, trademarks and copyrights can also be<br>described and registered.|
|3|**Easily replicable**|Hard to create the first copy, but copies cost next to nothing.|
|4|**Requires intellectual**<br>**effort**|Creations of the mind, but effort is needed – R&D, often**group efforts**<br>(co-authored papers, patents by multinational teams).|
|5|**Commercially valuable**|Can be used in trade and business.|



###### **Trait 2 in detail – registration and the copyright exception** 

The entire process from specification to grant is, in simple language, **registration** . There is an office/authority that analyses the right, scrutinises it, verifies it against other things and then grants a title. In patent law this is called **patent prosecution** , happening at the patent office; only after the written specification goes through prosecution is a **grant** (title of patent) made. 

**Copyright is the exception:** because of an international arrangement called the **Berne Convention** , **registration is not necessary to enforce a copyright** . Mere **publication with a copyright notice** (owner's name and year of publication) is good enough to claim ownership, though registration is still possible. 

###### **Trait 3 in detail – replication** 

- **Software example:** the first copy of Windows 7 cost a few million dollars (R&D, investment, staff salaries); every further copy costs next to nothing. 

- **Books:** years to write, minutes to scan and pirate on the cloud. 

- **Movies:** months/years to make; pirated versions appear within days of theatre release. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Five traits: **enforceable · registrable (describable) · easily replicable · requires intellectual effort · commercial value** . 

- **Copyright need not be registered** for enforcement (Berne Convention; © notice + name + year is enough). All other main IPR (patent, design, trademark) are registered. 

- Patent **prosecution** happens at the **patent office** ; the end result is a **grant** . 

- Remedies for infringement: **stopping the infringer** or claiming **damages (monetary compensation)** . 

###### **28.8 Economic Traits: Non-Rivalrous and Non-Excludable** 

Economists add two more terms to describe intellectual property. 

|**Term**|**Meaning**|**Illustration**|
|---|---|---|
|**Non-rivalrous**|Use of IP by one person does**not**<br>**take away**the enjoyment of the|**Room example:**a real room can be used in multiple ways<br>(bunk beds → classroom → social hall → party) but only up|
||same IP by another (no rivalry).|to a limit (~100 people at best; 500 people → police at the<br>door). A**bestselling book**can be read by a group around<br>one person, projected on a screen or put on the cloud to<br>be seen by millions of devices – no limit to enjoyment.|



Page 17 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Term**|**Meaning**|**Illustration**|
|---|---|---|
|**Non-excludable**|Once IP is available to some,<br>**others cannot be stopped**from|A book shown page-by-page in a**live YouTube stream**–<br>anyone with access can view it. A**lifesaving medicine**in|
||using it.|the market – competitors can analyse and copy it without<br>going to the manufacturer.|



Together, being **non-excludable and non-rivalrous** is a trait of what economists call **public goods** . Example: **national security** – nobody inside the country's borders can be excluded, and everybody gets its benefit. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Real property has **limits on enjoyment** ; IP has **none** (non-rivalrous). 

- **Public goods = non-excludable + non-rivalrous** (example: national security). 

- Non-rivalrous → use by one doesn't reduce use by another. Non-excludable → others can't be stopped once it is accessible. 

###### **28.9 Definitions of IPR and the WIPO Approach** 

There is **no agreed definition** of IPR. Dictionary-style definitions include: (a) rights that protect **applications of ideas and information of commercial value** ; (b) rights that protect **products of creative labour** . Some definitions simply **list** the rights ('grocery list' approach). 

**WIPO** = **World Intellectual Property Organization** , an international organisation **under the United Nations** that deals with IPR. Its definition is **expansive and list-like** : it includes patents, copyrights, trademarks, designs and similar rights, and speaks of both the things on which rights manifest and the actual rights. 

###### **Limitations of the WIPO (list-type) definition** 

1. It **does not take care of new and emerging IPR** (new rights are emerging continuously). 

2. It offers **no yardstick** to understand IPR – apart from referring to 'products of creative labour' it gives nothing for a uniform definition. 

**The 'human creative labour' problem.** Defining IPR by human creative labour has a difficulty: **geographical indications (GI)** involve no technical human creative effort. Yet emphasising human creative labour refers to the **origin** of IP, because IP originated through **copyrights and patents** , the two initial rights. 

###### **28.10 Types of IPR – First Overview** 

There are many types of IPR, distinguished by the nature of the product on which they manifest. 

|**Type**|**What it protects**|
|---|---|
|**Patents**|**Inventions**– technological inventions.|
|**Copyrights**|Literary and creative works – literary, artistic works,**sound tracks, videos,**<br>**cinematography, computer programs**and more.|
|**Designs**|What can be**distinguished by the eye**– something**visually appealing**but**not**<br>**functional**. Law of designs is different from the law of patents (which protect<br>functional things).|
|**Trademarks**|**Words and symbols**attributable to trade.|
|**Geographical**<br>**indications**|Indicate the**origin**of certain goods.|
|**Trade secrets**|Information relating to trade that the owner wants to keep**secret**.|



Page 18 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Patent → technological inventions (functional). Design → **visual appeal, no functional component** . 

- Copyright subject matter includes **computer programs** , sound tracks, videos, cinematography. 

- Trademark → words/symbols in trade. GI → origin of goods. Trade secrets → confidential trade information. 

- WIPO = **World Intellectual Property Organization** (UN). TRIPS and WTO appear in the next lecture.

###### **LECTURE 29** 

### **Defining IPR** 

Subject matter · Registration · Exclusive rights · Duration (limited vs unlimited life IP) · Patents, Copyright, Trademarks, Designs in detail 

###### **29.1 Defining IPR Through Four Elements** 

IPR is a **group of rights** that manifest on different expressions of ideas. To define it, four elements are examined one after the other: 

- **1 Subject matter** – what the right manifests itself on. 

- **2 Registration** – the form/process by which the right is recognised. 

- **3 The exclusive set of rights** – the bundle of rights given to the right holder. 

- **4 Duration** – how long the right lasts. 

Along with the traits seen earlier (the subject matter can be replicated, the right holder has the option of enforcing it against others, etc.), these elements together constitute the concept of IPR. 

###### **29.2 Element 1 – Subject Matter** 

An IPR is identified by the **subject matter** on which it manifests. Note that some newer rights are **not actually about ideas** – a geographical indication, for instance, is about products originating from a geography. 

|**If the idea / subject matter is…**|**…it is protected by**|
|---|---|
|An**invention**|**Patent**|
|An**expression**– a literary, artistic or cinematographic work|**Copyright**|
|An**aesthetic design**– pleases the eye,**no functional component**|**Registered design**|
|Words/symbols attributed to a trader|**Trademark**|
|Products valuable because of the**geography**they come from|**Geographical indication (GI)**|



###### **Geographical Indication (GI)** 

**Simple language.** Certain products are valuable because they come from a particular place whose weather or climate contributes to the product. The product is identified with that place – there is not much 'idea' involved; it is an **attribution to origin** . 

- **Beneficiaries:** the **community of producers** from that place who manufacture/produce the product – they alone can use the GI label. 

- **Example: Darjeeling tea** – plantation owners and producers in Darjeeling can use the label because that tea has properties not found in tea grown in **Coorg** or parts of **Sri Lanka** . 

###### **Registered Design – the 'eye test'** 

A **registered design** covers things that are **aesthetically pleasing to the eye** ; the test is **what the eye can see** . 

|**Example**|**Functional element?**|**Result**|
|---|---|---|
|**Sports shoe**(Nike / Adidas) – shape cuts air|**Yes**– design elements also|Right holders do**not**normally go|
|faster, gives grip, supports parts of the foot|contribute to functionality|for a registered design; they rely<br>on other rights (see below).|



Page 20 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Example**|**Functional element?**|**Result**|
|---|---|---|
|**Children's shoe designed like a rabbit**or a|**No**– purely appeals to the|Suitable subject matter for a|
|cartoon character|eye|**registered design**.|



When functional elements are tied to the design, right holders have **multiple rights** available: a **trademark** , and **copyright** on the way it looks (almost all Disney characters were at some point subject matter of copyright; you cannot create that image in any other form without violating the copyright). **Copyright in India lasts for the life of the author plus 60 years** , a substantial right. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Registered design = **aesthetic, eye-appealing, no functional component** ; if function is tied in → other rights (patent, trademark, copyright). 

- GI beneficiaries = the **community of producers** from that place (e.g. Darjeeling tea) – **not** a single owner. 

- GI is **not based on an idea** – it is an attribution of origin. 

- Subject matter first, then form of protection: invention → patent; expression → copyright; aesthetic design → registered design. 

###### **29.3 Element 2 – Registration** 

**Why registration?** IPR are **intangible** , so their boundaries are hard to see. **Registration** is the way to understand **what the right holder has claimed** . It makes the right **officially recognised** , verifiable by anyone, and backed by law. Registration is done by the **government/state** and **confers sanctity** on the IPR. 

|**Right**|**What is registered**|**Law that lets holder enforce**<br>**it**|
|---|---|---|
|Patent|Patent specification → patent granted|**Patents Act, 1970**|
|Trademark|Marks (forms and figures filed)|**Trademarks Act, 1999**|
|Copyright|Literary works (can be registered,**not mandatory for**<br>**enforcement**);**software code is registered by way of**<br>**copyright**|—|
|Design|Design (forms and figures)|—|



###### **Simple process: Trademark and Design registration** 

- **1** File **forms and figures** (papers with the mark/design). 

- **2** The **registry does a check** – essentially **matching what has gone before** . 

- **3** If **similar terms** exist, the registry may **raise an objection** . 

- **4** If you coined a word for the first time / the design is unique, original and you are the **first to file** , you get the registration. A **straightforward process** . 

###### **Detailed process: Patents** 

A patent specification explains the **entire invention** , not only what it is: its **working, features, advantages and variations** . The right holder also explains **what went before** – the background art – and why it was not possible for a **person skilled in the art** to come up with the invention, whereas the inventor, by **inventive effort** , did. 

###### **Structure of a patent specification (a narrative):** 

- **1 Background art** – technologies that existed before. 

- **2 Description of the invention** , its **purposes** and the **problems it solves** . 

- **3 Embodiments and illustrations** – how the invention works; parts (for a mechanical invention). 

- **4 Claims** – the concluding part. 

###### **Examination and prosecution in India** 

Page 21 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

- **1** The **Patent Office** does not merely register; it **scrutinises the details in great depth** . 

- **2** A report called the **First Examination Report** is given to the right holder asking how the invention can be justified against any objections (most likely there are some). 

- **3** Objections may be **technical** or **substantial** . Substantial objections arise if: (a) the subject matter is **not patentable under Indian law** ; (b) it is already **preceded in the art** (not novel); (c) it is something **obvious** to everybody. 

- **4** Overcoming these objections takes time. The whole process of the office raising objections and the applicant responding is called **patent prosecution** . 

|**Aspect**|**Trademark / Design**|**Patent**|
|---|---|---|
|Filing|Forms and figures|Detailed specification narrative|
|Office check|Matching against earlier marks/designs<br>(similar terms → objection)|Deep scrutiny;**First Examination Report**;<br>substantial objections (non-patentable subject<br>matter, prior art, obviousness)|
|Complexity|Straightforward|**Very detailed and sometimes complicated**–<br>patent prosecution|
|Ends with|Registration|**Claims**define the right; grant after prosecution|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Trademarks Act = **1999** ; Patents Act = **1970** . 

- Registration is done by the **government/state** ; registration process **varies** with the type of IPR. 

- Patent specification order: **background art → invention/purposes/problems solved → embodiments & illustrations → claims (last)** . 

- Substantial objections: **non-patentable subject matter, already in prior art, obvious** . 

- Software code is registered via **copyright** ; copyright registration is **not mandatory** to enforce. 

- In India the first report after filing is the **First Examination Report** . 

###### **29.4 Copyright and the Oral-Formulaic Tradition; Why IPR Arose with the Industrial Revolution** 

**Copyright is the right to make copies.** Making copies implies a **medium** – paper for print; tape or digital for film. Reciting or memorising a poem is not 'making a copy'; it is 'learning by heart'. 

**Oral-formulaic tradition.** Before copyright became a recognised right (just before the industrial revolution, especially in Europe), knowledge was transmitted by **memorising and passing on to the next generation** (as with nursery rhymes). Since the medium is the mind, this tradition **stays outside the purview of copyright** , because copyright requires the work to be **recorded or expressed in some medium** . Studies suggest **Shakespeare** inherited from this tradition; some of what he wrote already existed in it. 

**Why the Industrial Revolution is seen as the take-off point of IPR** 

|**Factor**|**How it contributed**|
|---|---|
|**Mechanised manufacturing**|Producing many copies became easier – matching the trait that IP is replicable.|
|**Value and marketing**|Items manufactured in large numbers gained value; an entire marketing<br>industry arose – matching the trait that IPR are commercially valuable.|
|**Advances in printing**<br>**technology**|Quick spread of ideas; great works could be copied and sent to other places.|
|**Steel and long-voyage ships**|People crossed borders faster and in larger numbers.|
|**Translation**|Need to translate works (e.g. a German philosopher's or artist's work spread in<br>England); translation/interpretation grew and ideas spread further.|



Page 22 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

Rights existed in remote instances earlier (some kings granted them), but the rights attained **international recognition with the industrial revolution** , because IPR are tied to ideas and to the **dissemination of ideas** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Copyright = the right to make **copies** ; a copy needs a **medium** . 

- **Oral-formulaic tradition** (memorising and passing on) is **outside** the copyright regime unless captured in some medium. 

- IPR gained international recognition with the **industrial revolution** (printing, steel/ships, translation, mechanised copies, marketing). 

###### 

###### **29.5 Element 3 – The Exclusive Set of Rights (Bundle of Rights)** 

Every IPR gives a **bundle of exclusive rights** , and the bundle **varies** by type of IPR because subject matter varies. 

|**IPR**|**Exclusive rights conferred**|
|---|---|
|**Copyright**|For a fixed number of years: right to**print, publish, perform, film or record**literary,<br>artistic, musical or cinematographic works – i.e. making more copies in different forms.|
|**Trademark**|Right to use a**symbol or word**that is**legally registered or established by use**to<br>represent a company's**products or services**. Can be used for all products and new<br>industries; can stop others**even if the owner has no products there**, because the mark<br>carries**goodwill**.|
|**Patent**|Right to**make, sell, use, offer for sale and import**the invention – manufacture,<br>marketing, sale and importation.|



**Case example – Benz Tristar logo.** Someone used the Mercedes-Benz **Tristar** logo for **undergarments in India** . Benz had no known intention to enter the garment business, but the court **stopped the use** : a right holder can stop others even in a business he is not in, **especially if the mark is well known and reputed** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Copyright bundle = print, publish, perform, film, record. Patent bundle = make, sell, use, offer for sale, import. Trademark = use of registered/established symbol or word for products/services. 

- Trademark holder can stop use **even in other industries** if the mark is well known (Benz Tristar – undergarments). 

- Trademark rights can arise from **registration or from use** . 

###### 

###### **29.6 Element 4 – Duration: Limited-Life vs Unlimited-Life IP** 

The duration of rights also differs. From a **business perspective** , all IP falls into two categories (true across the globe): 

|**Category**|**Meaning**|**IPR in this category**|
|---|---|---|
|**Limited-life IP**|Duration is limited; afterwards it falls into the<br>**public domain**and anyone can use it.|**Patents, copyrights**(design rights<br>too are of limited life)|
|**Unlimited-life IP**|Subject to renewal/conditions, can be kept alive<br>**forever**.|**Trademarks, trade secrets**|



###### **Trademark duration** 

Trademarks are for a **fixed term but renewable as long as the right holder wishes** (e.g. the Coca-Cola mark is close to 100 years old or more). In business parlance they are **unlimited-life IP** . The holder must do **two things** : 

- **1 Pay the official fees** (renewal charges) to the government. 

Page 23 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

- **2 Be vigilant** – take action against people using the mark without consent; otherwise it may be assumed that the right has been given up. 

If the mark is **not renewed or not used** , it may not be enforceable. The course material states that a trademark is **granted for 10 years** and can be renewed; a 5-year renewal interval is also mentioned in it, but the standard Indian renewal period is 10 years – if an exam option is unclear, prefer **10 years** . 

###### **Coca-Cola illustration** 

Coca-Cola combines multiple rights: **design rights** on bottles (limited life – come and go), **copyright** on the way products are displayed (artistic work), and a **trademark on the word** (unlimited life). The way to carry a business forward is to hold an **unlimited-life IP** . Old designs that are not renewed can be used by others, but the **mark** can always and only be used by the right holder. 

###### **Trade secrets** 

A trade secret is **unlimited-life** IP: if it can be kept **confidential** , it can be enforced **as long as it remains confidential** . 

###### **Patent duration and the TRIPS Agreement** 

**DURATION AT A GLANCE Patent term = 20 years from the date of application Copyright term (India) = life of the author + 60 years Trademark = granted for 10 years, renewable indefinitely** 

- **TRIPS** = Agreement on **Trade-Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights** , an agreement under the **World Trade Organization (WTO)** . **Every WTO member is bound to implement it** ; hence across the board the patent term is **20 years** . 

- **Before TRIPS:** in India the term used to be **14 years** ; for **pharmaceuticals up to 7 years** ; for food and agricultural products even less – the term was flexible. The predominant term across the globe before 20 years was **14 years** . 

- The 20-year term is **technology-agnostic** (not technology-specific): it applies to pharmaceuticals, aeronautics, biotechnology, software (in countries that grant software patents), even for a technology with a 3-year life span. 20 years does not make sense for all technologies (some technologies change many generations within 20 years). 

- **Origin of 20 years:** international lobbying during the **Uruguay Round** (about **8 years of negotiations** ) before the WTO was formed. 

###### **Historical explanation of the earlier 14-year term** 

- **1** Early British monarchs granted **exclusive privileges** , not patents, to attract talented craftsmen from Continental Europe (soap, glassware, perfume, playing cards etc.). 

- **2** Without protection, a craftsman's trade would be copied immediately, so a **royal privilege** gave protection to people with special skills (also given for mineral exploration; the **East India Company** came with an exclusive charter). 

- **3** In return, the craftsman had to **train two British nationals** – a way of **skill (technology) transfer** . 

- **4** An apprenticeship took **7 years** ; **7 + 7 = 14 years** , which explains the 14-year term (one explanation offered). 

###### **Copyright duration** 

- India: **life of the author + 60 years** – from date of creation till the author's death and 60 years after. An author who writes early and lives long gets a longer right. 

- Example: works of **Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi** came into the **public domain around 2009** (life + 60 years expired). 

Page 24 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

- If the creator is an **institution** : date of creation/publication **+ 60 years** . 

- **No TRIPS-like mandate for copyright** , so the term **varies between countries** (some 50 years, some more than 60, the US more than that). 

|**IPR**|**Term**|**Category**|
|---|---|---|
|Patent|**20 years from date of application**(TRIPS, all WTO<br>members)|Limited life|
|Copyright (India)|**Life of author + 60 years**; institution: 60 years|Limited life|
|Trademark|Granted for**10 years**; renewable as long as the holder<br>wishes|**Unlimited life**|
|Trade secret|As long as kept**confidential**|**Unlimited life**|
|Design|Limited life (right 'comes and goes')|Limited life|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- **Patent: 20 years from the date of** **_application_** (not from grant). Same in all WTO members regardless of technology. 

- Pre-TRIPS: India 14 years; pharmaceuticals up to **7 years** . 

- Copyright term is **not harmonised** by TRIPS-like mandate → varies by country. India: **life + 60 years** . 

- Trademarks and trade secrets = **unlimited-life IP** ; patents and copyrights = **limited-life IP** (then public domain). 

- Trademark holder's two duties: **pay official fees** + **be vigilant against unauthorised use** . 

- A **rebranded/discontinued mark is not open to others** if the owner keeps it alive – unlike an expired patent, which becomes free for everyone. 

- Trade secrets last **only while confidential** . 

###### **29.7 Quick Run-Through of Each IPR** 

###### **A. Patents** 

- **Exclusive right conferred by the government.** Exclusivity = ability to stop others. 

- Right pertains to **making, selling, using, offering for sale and importing** . 

- Lasts **20 years from the date of application** ; afterwards the subject matter falls into the **public domain** and is free for everybody. 

- **Medical methods of treatment** are **not patentable** in India – **Section 3** of the Indian Patents Act does not allow methods of treatment to be protected. 

###### **B. Copyright** 

- An **exclusive right** , conferred by the government when registration is sought, **or upon creation** – by publishing with the **copyright notice** : © symbol, name and year of publication. 

- **Registration is not required for enforcement** (e.g. books in a library carry only a copyright note). **Many websites** carry a copyright notice in the footer – meaning content should be reproduced only with permission. 

- Purpose of a website's copyright notice: to prevent people copying content en masse and passing it off as somebody else's website. One can still **hyperlink** to a website without copying; the **logo is protected by trademark** . 

- Rights pertain to print, publish, perform, record. Duration: **life of author + 60 years** ; institution: creation + 60 years. 

|**Question**|**Answer**|
|---|---|
|Can the same film name (e.g. 'Don') be reused by|That does**not**come under copyright; it is a separate matter|
|another film?|(industry arrangements / other statutes).|



Page 25 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Question**|**Answer**|
|---|---|
|Where are company names registered?|Not under copyright – the**Companies Act**has a norm for<br>registering company names.|
|Where are domain names registered?|Not under copyright – through a**domain registration**<br>**service**; the name is held as long as it is renewed.|



###### **C. Trademarks** 

- **Exclusive right, conferred by the government** – registered, **or established by use** . 

- **Established by use – IKEA example:** a mark used in Europe but not sold in India; if an Indian manufacturer started using it, the owner could still stop him because the right is established by **international use and reputation** ; the **law of passing off** helps. 

- **Law of passing off** protects a mark that is **used in trade and has reputation even though unregistered** (e.g. old businesses that registered only later; a generation later they register but could stop others earlier through use). 

- Right expresses itself in **symbols, words and marks** that identify the producer and origin of goods and services (e.g. the Tata mark on salt, automobiles, TCS). 

- Without respect for marks: **piracy and counterfeit** , doubts about product reliability, damage to company reputation. Marks are the **'ambassadors'** of a business. 

- The mark holder can stop others **even if the others give equal quality** . 

- **Rebranding:** an old mark is not open to others when discontinued – the owner still keeps it alive (e.g. **Airtel** changed from a word logo to a symbol but can still stop others from using the old mark; the **word** 'Airtel' in any font is covered). 

- Contrast: a **patent** that expires or is revoked can be used by everybody. 

###### **D. Designs** 

- **Exclusive right, conferred by the government → involves registration.** 

- Pertains to **shape, configuration, pattern** etc. that are **aesthetic** , pleasing to the eye, with **no functional element** (if functional, it goes to the domain of patents). 

- If something can be protected by **copyright** (an artistic work), copyright is preferred since its term (life + 60) is longer – nobody goes for a lesser term. 

- Design law is for **industrial designs** , not artistic works: e.g. interlocking tiles, TMT steel bars, shape of a bottle – **mass-produced goods** , dresses, jewellery. 

- It is the **weakest of intellectual property rights** – there are restrictions on enforcement and damages are limited. 

- **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Copyright comes into effect **upon registration or upon creation with notice** ; **enforcement does not need registration** . 

- Film titles, company names and domain names are **not copyright subject matter** (Companies Act / domain registration). 

- A hyperlink does not copy content; a **logo** is protected by **trademark** , not copyright. 

- Trademark can be **established by use** (IKEA) – **law of passing off** helps even without registration/business in the country. 

- Methods of medical treatment are **excluded** from patent protection under **Section 3** of the Indian Act. 

- • Designs: **weakest IPR** , limited damages; for **mass-produced industrial** articles; purely **aesthetic** . 

- Patent = free for all after expiry/revocation; old trademarks stay protected as long as kept alive. 

Page 26 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **29.8 Master Comparison Table – Four Main IPR** 

|**Feature**|**Patent**|**Copyright**|**Trademark**|**Design**|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|Subject<br>matter|Invention<br>(technological,<br>functional)|Literary, artistic,<br>musical,<br>cinematographic<br>works, software|Words/symbols<br>indicating trade origin|Aesthetic shape,<br>configuration, pattern –<br>no function|
|Registration|Yes – detailed<br>specification; patent<br>prosecution; First<br>Examination Report|Optional (not<br>required to enforce);<br>© notice|Yes, or established by<br>use (passing off)|Yes – filing forms and<br>figures|
|Exclusive<br>rights|Make, sell, use, offer<br>for sale, import|Print, publish,<br>perform, film, record|Use of the symbol/word<br>for products or services;<br>stop others (even in<br>other fields if well<br>known)|Stop copying of the<br>registered visual design<br>(weakest right)|

|After term|Public domain|Public domain|Stays while renewed<br>and policed|Public domain|

###### **LECTURE 30** 

### **Design of Experiments – Introduction** 

Why experiment · Scatter & random error · Random variables · PDF/CDF · Mean & variance · Normal distribution · Population & sampling 

###### **30.1 Recommended Textbooks for Design of Experiments** 

|**Book**|**Level / use**|
|---|---|
|Montgomery & Runger,_Applied Statistics and Probability for_<br>_Engineers_, 5th ed., John Wiley India, New Delhi, 2011|**Basic**book – starts from statistics, goes on to<br>probability, and later to**design of experiments**<br>**and regression**. Recommended for**getting**<br>**started**.|
|Montgomery,_Design and Analysis of Experiments_|Various possible designs, their**implementation**<br>**and analysis**. For implementing DoE in your own<br>experimental work.|
|Myers et al. (more advanced text)|Requires a background in**linear algebra**; for<br>theoretical background and advanced DoE<br>options.|
|Ogunnaike (new book)|Handles some complex topics nicely; has<br>interesting examples.|



**Suggested path:** first book to get started → once interested and wanting to implement DoE in experiments, the Montgomery DoE book → for deeper theory and advanced options, the Myers et al. text. 

###### **30.2 Why Do We Do Experiments, and What Does DoE Offer?** 

**Simple language.** Experiments are done to check ideas against reality. DoE helps to plan experiments and report their outcomes in a scientifically acceptable way, so that the frustration of scattered results is overcome. 

###### **Reasons for experiments:** 

- To **prove a theory** . 

- When **theory is not developed** or is very difficult to develop for a particular process or application. 

- As **proof of concept** . 

###### **The experimental workflow DoE supports** 

- **1 Represent the experimental data** using **histograms, box charts, scatter plots, normal probability plots** , etc. 

- **2 Repeat** experiments to confirm results are more or less the same each time, and present the data in **average form** (average of all responses). 

- **3 Quantify the scatter** (deviation between repeats) in a suitable way – identical outputs are very unlikely. 

- **4** Find **which variables actually influence** the experiment. A variable with **no significant effect** can be **fixed at a certain level** , reducing the number of further experiments. 

- **5** Perform a basic set of experiments called **screening experiments** . 

- **6** Use the knowledge to decide **where to do the next set of experiments** – explore the **experimental design space** and quickly lead to **optimum conditions** (e.g. **maximum yield or minimum power** ). 

Page 28 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Screening experiments** = the basic first set of experiments used to find which variables matter. 

- A variable with no significant effect → **keep it fixed** → fewer experiments. 

- Data representation tools: **histogram, box chart, scatter plot, normal probability plot** . 

- Objective of later experiments: reach optimum conditions ( **maximum yield, minimum power** ). 

###### **30.3 Variability (Scatter) in Experimental Data** 

**Scatter is inevitable.** Many **random effects** influence an experiment and they cannot all be controlled. So one should not be frustrated when repeats do not give identical responses, nor conclude that the experimentation is wrong. One has to **live with the scatter and still draw meaningful conclusions** . Along with averaging, importance must be given to the **variability** in the data. 

DoE accounts for **random variability** and for the **systematic variability** caused by changing the variables, and compares the differences between the two. 

###### **The basic model of a response** 

###### **RESPONSE MODEL** 

###### **yi = ηi + εi** 

|**Symbol**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|yi|Response (output) of the**i-th run**.|
|ηi(eta)|The**true (absolute) value**– unfortunately**not known**; must be**estimated**by<br>experiments while living with the variability.|
|εi(epsilon)|The**random error component**; it may be**positive or negative**, so responses vary<br>on both sides.|



**Example.** Repeating an experiment may give 100 the first time, 101 the second time, 97 the third time – variation on either side because εi is positive or negative. **If εi were 0** , and every component of the setup worked perfectly, repeats would give the **same response** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Scatter in repeated experiments is **inevitable** (random effects cannot all be controlled) – it does **not** mean the experiment is wrong. 

- In yi = ηi + εi: **η = true value (unknown)** , **ε = random error (±)** . 

- Averaging alone is **not** enough – **variability must be quantified** . 

- DoE separates and compares **random** variability vs **systematic** variability (caused by changing variables). 

###### **30.4 Why Basic Statistical Concepts First?** 

Time must be invested in basic concepts, otherwise terms such as **P-value, confidence intervals, level of significance and mean square error** cannot be understood and one cannot know what the results are telling. (These terms are to be understood through the basics that follow.) 

###### **30.5 Random Variable and Probability Distribution** 

**Simple language.** A random variable is an abstract quantity whose value depends on chance. Before the experiment you talk about the variable in general; after the experiment you have a particular value. 

|**Notation**|**Meaning**|
|---|---|
|**X**(capital)|The**random variable**(abstract entity).|
|**x**(small)|The**value of the random variable once known**after the experiment.|



The random variable is the **originator of probability distribution functions** , which give the **distribution of the random variable** . Distributions are used to **predict the behaviour of a group of** 

Page 29 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **randomly behaving entities** . 

**Example – a class.** Predicting one student's performance is very difficult; but for a group, collective behaviour is easier to predict because collections of individuals follow one distribution or another. An experienced teacher may say: about **10%** of students will do very well, about **5%** pretty badly, and the **average** performance is likely around **60%** . 

**Continuous vs discrete:** random variables may be continuous or discrete, but in design of experiments we usually deal with **continuously varying quantities** . The **probability density function** describes the distribution of probabilities in the continuous random-variable domain. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- **Capital X = random variable; small x = its observed value** . 

- Distributions predict **group** (collective) behaviour better than individual behaviour. 

- DoE normally deals with **continuous** random variables. 

###### **30.6 Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF)** 

**Definition.** The cumulative distribution function F(z) is the probability that the random variable takes a value **less than or equal to z** , where z is any particular value (0.5, 1, 3 or even infinity). It is obtained by integrating the probability density function f(x) from −∞ up to z – analytically if the form of f(x) permits, or numerically if f(x) is complex. 

###### **PROBABILITY & CUMULATIVE FORMULAS** 

**F(z) = P(X ≤ z) = ∫−∞z f(x) dx As z → +∞ : F(∞) = ∫−∞∞ f(x) dx = 1 (total area under the curve = sum of probabilities = 1) b f(x) dx = F(b) − F(a) P(a ≤ X ≤ b) = ∫a** 

###### **Step-by-step: how to read a CDF value** 

- **1** Choose the upper limit z. 

- **2** Integrate f(x) from −∞ to z (analytically or numerically). 

- **3** The result is the **cumulative probability** up to z; at z = +∞ it equals **1** . 

- **4** For an interval a to b: compute **F(b) − F(a)** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Total area under any probability density curve = **1** . 

- **F(z) = P(X ≤ z)** – 'less than or equal to'. 

- P(a ≤ X ≤ b) = **F(b) − F(a)** (upper minus lower). 

- We **integrate** rather than add, because X is continuous. 

###### **30.7 Mean and Variance of a Distribution** 

**Simple language.** A distribution has a **centre** (average value) and a **spread** . The mean tells where the centre is; the variance tells how widely it spreads. 

|**Quantity**|**Symbol**|**Definition (continuous distribution)**|
|---|---|---|
|**Mean**|μ|μ = E(X) = ∫−∞<br>∞x f(x) dx|
|**Variance**|σ<sup>2</sup>|σ<sup>2</sup>= E[(X − μ)<sup>2</sup>] = ∫−∞<br>∞(x − μ)2f(x) dx|
|**Standard deviation**|σ|σ = √(variance) (always positive)|



- **Mean** represents the average value; in symmetric distributions it may be at the geometric centre, but in **skewed** distributions it may not be at the centre. 

Page 30 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

- ∫ f(x) dx = 1, but **∫ x f(x) dx need not equal 1** – it can be any value, even **negative** if the distribution lies mostly on the negative side of the x-axis. 

- Variance measures the deviation of the random variable from the mean, **squared** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- E(X) = μ = ∫ x f(x) dx (mean) – **can be negative** . 

- Variance σ² = E[(X − μ)²]; standard deviation σ = **square root of variance** . 

- Only **∫ f(x) dx = 1** ; the mean integral is not restricted to 1. 

###### **30.8 The Normal Distribution** 

The **normal distribution** is one of the most important and popular probability density functions. It has wide applications in science and engineering, and many other distributions tend to it under certain conditions – e.g. the **t-distribution tends to the normal distribution when the degrees of freedom tend to infinity** . 

**Parameters.** In the normal distribution the parameters of the distribution are the **mean (μ)** and the **standard deviation (σ)** themselves. (In other distributions, parameters such as a and b must be manipulated mathematically to get the mean and standard deviation.) Changing μ and σ gives different normal distributions – hence they are called **parameters** . 

**NORMAL PROBABILITY DENSITY FUNCTION f(x) = [ 1 / √(2π σ**<sup>**2**</sup> **) ] · exp[ − (x − μ)**<sup>**2**</sup> **/ (2σ**<sup>**2**</sup> **) ] Notation: X ~ N(μ, σ**<sup>**2**</sup> **) (μ = mean, σ**<sup>**2**</sup> **= variance)** 

###### **Shape and properties** 

- **Symmetrical** bell shape, centred at the mean μ (also written x̄ when it is the sample mean). 

- **50%** of the area lies below the mean and **50%** above; total area = 100% = probability **1** . The tails become very thin far from the mean (range −∞ to +∞). 

- **Mean = median = mode** ; its properties are well understood and elegant to work with. 

- The mean μ **may be negative** (centre can be at any value, e.g. −5 or +10); the variance σ² and standard deviation σ are **always positive** . 

###### **Normal distribution N(μ, σ²) – area within 1, 2 and 3 standard deviations of the mean** 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
≈ 68%<br>≈ 99% (μ ± 3σ) ≈ 99% (μ ± 3σ)<br>≈ 95% (μ ± 2σ) ≈ 95% (μ ± 2σ)<br>μ−3σ μ−2σ μ−σ μ μ+σ μ+2σ μ+3σ<br><!-- End of picture text -->

|**Range around the mean**|**Area under the curve (fraction of data)**|
|---|---|
|μ ± 1σ|**≈ 68%**(more exactly 68.2% = 34.1% + 34.1%)|
|μ ± 2σ|**≈ 95%**|
|μ ± 3σ|**≈ 99%**|



Page 31 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Normal distribution is **symmetric** ; **mean = median = mode** . 

- **68% – 95% – 99%** for 1σ, 2σ, 3σ on **either side** of the mean (68% means total of both sides). 

- Parameters of the normal distribution = **μ and σ** directly (unlike distributions with parameters a and b). 

- **σ is always positive; μ may be negative.** 

- **t-distribution → normal** as degrees of freedom → ∞. 

- Notation: **N(μ, σ²)** – second argument is the **variance** , not σ. 

###### **30.9 Standardisation and the Standard Normal Distribution** 

**Why?** Infinitely many normal distributions exist depending on μ and σ². To avoid separate tables for each, any normal variable is converted to one **standard** form, so that only a **single table** of probabilities is needed. 

###### **Step-by-step standardisation** 

- **1** Start with any normal random variable X with mean μ and standard deviation σ. 

- **2 Subtract the mean** μ from X. 

- **3 Divide** the difference by the standard deviation σ. 

- **4** The result Z is the **standard normal random variable** : centred at **0** with **unit standard deviation (variance = 1)** . 

- **5** Read probabilities from the single table of the standard normal distribution. 

###### **STANDARDISATION** 

###### **Z = (X − μ) / σ → Z ~ N(0, 1)** 

###### **Φ(z) = P(Z ≤ z) (cumulative distribution of the standard normal)** 

**Standard normal curve areas.** The curve is centred at **0** ; σ = **1** . A point at ±0.5 is half a standard deviation from the mean. The area from the mean to 0.5σ is **19.1%** , and the area from 0.5σ to 1σ is **15%** , so the area from the mean to 1σ is 15 + 19.1 = **34.1%** on each side. Thus **68.2%** of the area lies within one standard deviation on either side of the mean (about 68%). 

|**Interval on Z**|**Area**|
|---|---|
|0 to 0.5|19.1%|
|0.5 to 1|15% (approx.)|
|0 to 1 (one side)|34.1%|
|−1 to +1 (both sides)|68.2%|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Standardising = **subtract mean, then divide by standard deviation** : Z = (X − μ)/σ. 

- Standard normal: **mean 0, variance 1 (σ = 1)** . 

- Capital Z is the **random variable** ; small z is a **numerical value** . Φ(z) = P(Z ≤ z). 

- 34.1% on each side of the mean within 1σ → **68.2%** in total. 

###### **30.10 Population, Sample and Random Sampling** 

It would be ideal if data were normally distributed, but for a large data set we often **do not know the shape or the distribution** it follows. This leads to the idea of a population. 

Page 32 

Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide · Lectures 27–30 

|**Term**|**Meaning**|**Examples / purpose**|
|---|---|---|
|**Population**|The large collection of entities whose<br>collective behaviour one wishes to<br>understand.|Student population, population of people preferring<br>a particular soft drink. Needed for<br>**decision-making, quality control and**<br>**marketing**; helps set goals, objectives, process<br>and settings.|
|**Sample**|A subset taken from the population –<br>because collecting data from**every**<br>**entity is practically impossible**.|Used to estimate the population mean, variance<br>and distribution. For a reliable estimate the sample<br>must be**drawn carefully**.|



###### **Criteria for a good (random) sample** 

- **1** The sampled observations must be **independent of each other** . 

- **2** Each entity must have an **equal probability of being picked** . 

- **3** All sampled elements must **represent the same probability distribution** (come under the same distribution). 

- **4 More sample elements** → more confidence and greater precision in the conclusions. 

**Example – box of 1000 balls.** To know the distribution of colours, if all blue balls are packed in the top layer and you pick 10 from the top, you may wrongly conclude that all balls are blue – red and white balls had no chance of being picked. If the balls are **randomly mixed** before picking, a sample of 10 gives a reasonable picture; the **more balls picked, the better the idea** . 

###### **Statistics** 

Sample properties of interest are the **sample mean** and **sample variance** , and they are themselves **random variables** . If the entities of a sample are random variables, **any mathematical combination of them is also a random variable** ; such **functions of random variables are called statistics** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS – Always / Never / Only when** 

- Random sample: **independent · equally likely · same distribution** ; larger n → more precise. 

- **Statistic** = a function of random variables (e.g. sample mean, sample variance) – itself a **random variable** . 

- Population is studied through **samples** because measuring every entity is impractical. 

- Biased picking (e.g. only top-layer balls) violates equal probability and misleads the conclusion.

|Standardisation|Z = (X − μ)/σ → mean 0, variance 1.|
|Sampling|Independent, equal chance, same distribution; more samples → more precision; statistics<br>= functions of random variables.|

###### **Cross-Lecture 'Never / Always / Only when' Rules** 

###### **ALWAYS / NEVER / ONLY WHEN** 

- **Never** avoid all risk – manage it ( **risk aware, not risk averse** ). 

- **Always** obtain voluntary, informed consent from human participants; participants may **withdraw at any stage** . 

- **Never** disclose identifiable participant data without consent; **anonymise where appropriate** . 

- **Only when necessary** may animals be used in research. 

- **Authorship only for substantial contribution** ; otherwise acknowledge. 

- **Never** protects ideas per se – IPR protect **applications/expressions** of ideas. 

- Patent protection is confined to the **claims** . 

- Copyright **need not be registered** to be enforced. 

- Trademark holder must **pay fees** and **police the mark** to keep it alive. 

- Registered design: **only** when the feature is purely aesthetic (no function). 

- Medical methods of treatment: **not patentable** in India (Section 3). 

- Repeated experiments will **never** be exactly identical – scatter is inevitable (random error ε). 

- Total area under a probability density curve is **always 1** ; **σ is always positive** , μ may be negative. 

- A random sample needs **independence, equal probability and a common distribution** .

##### **Chapter 1 · Samples, Estimators and Degrees of Freedom** 

_Covers: Lecture 34 (first half)_ 

###### **1.1 Why we sample** 

A **population** is the complete collection of all the values you would like to know about. Measuring all of it is usually impossible, so you measure a small part of it, called a **sample** , and use that sample to learn about the population. Numbers that describe the population are called **population parameters** (the population mean **μ** and the population variance **σ²** ). Numbers calculated from the sample are used to _estimate_ those parameters. 

###### **1.2 Sample mean and sample variance** 

The two most common summaries of a sample are its average (sample mean) and its spread (sample variance). 

**Sample mean: x̄ = ( Σ xi ) / n (sum over i = 1 to n)** 

**Sample variance: s² = Σ ( xi − x̄ )² / ( n − 1 )** 

Here **n** is the sample size and **xi** is the i-th measurement. In s², each deviation from the mean is **squared** and then summed, so that positive and negative deviations do not cancel each other. 

###### **1.3 Degrees of freedom — why divide by (n − 1)?** 

**Degrees of freedom (df)** = the number of _independent_ entities in the collection you are looking at. 

**Simple reasoning.** There are n deviations (xi − x̄). But the deviations of any set of numbers from their own mean always add up to exactly zero. So once you know n − 1 of the deviations, the last one is forced (it must make the total zero). Only **n − 1** deviations are free to vary independently. That is why s² has **n − 1 degrees of freedom** . 

1. Take n measurements and compute x̄. 

2. Form the n deviations xi − x̄. 

3. Observe the constraint: Σ (xi − x̄) = 0. 

4. Therefore only n − 1 deviations are independent → df = n − 1 → divide the sum of squares by n − 1. 

###### **1.4 Estimator vs estimate; point vs interval** 

Before the sample is actually taken, x̄ and s² are formulas made of random variables — they are called **estimators** of the population mean and variance. After the sample is taken and numbers are plugged in, the resulting values (written with small letters, x̄ and s²) are called **sample estimates** . Because each is one single number, they are also called **point estimates** . 

A different kind of answer is an **interval estimate** : instead of one number, you give a range in which the parameter is believed to lie. 

###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

- **Train example.** You ask villagers when the daily train arrives. One person says “2:10 pm” — a **point estimate** (single value). Another says “between 2:00 pm and 2:30 pm” — an **interval estimate** (a range). 

###### **1.5 Sampling distribution (preview)** 

— 3 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

The random variable x has its own probability distribution (it can take a spectrum of values). Anything calculated from x — such as x̄ or s² — is also a random variable, so it too has a probability distribution. These are called the **sampling distribution of the sample mean** and the **sampling distribution of the sample variance** . Chapter 2 studies them. 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Sample mean** is denoted x̄ ; **sample variance** is denoted s². 

- x̄ and s² are **estimators** of the population mean μ and variance σ² respectively. 

- A value computed from an actual sample is a **sample estimate** = **point estimate** (a single value). 

- **Degrees of freedom** = number of independent entities in a collection; for the sample variance it is **n − 1** . 

- An **interval estimate** gives a range of values instead of a single value. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- The denominator of the sample variance is **n − 1** , **not n** . 

- The sum of deviations from the sample mean is **always zero** — this is the reason for the loss of one degree of freedom. 

- “Estimator” = the rule/formula (random variable); “estimate” = the number obtained from one actual sample. 

- Different samples from the same population generally give **different** x̄ values. 

— 4 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 2 · Sampling Distribution of the Mean and the Central Limit Theorem** 

_Covers: Lecture 34 (middle part)_ 

###### **2.1 What is a “sampling distribution of the mean”?** 

Suppose you draw sample after sample from the same population. The first sample will give one mean, the second a slightly different mean, and so on. These many sample means themselves form a distribution — the **sampling distribution of the mean** — which has its own centre (mean) and its own spread (variance). 

**Set-up assumed:** n independent random variables x1, x2, …, xn, all coming from populations with the **same mean μ** and the **same variance σ²** . 

###### **2.2 Centre of the sampling distribution** 

Each xi has expected value μ. Since x̄ = (x1 + … + xn)/n, its expected value is also μ. 

**E( x̄ ) = μ (the “mean of the means” equals the population mean)** 

###### **2.3 Spread of the sampling distribution** 

One might guess that V(x̄) is also σ², but it is not. Step-by-step: 

1. x̄ = x1/n + x2/n + … + xn/n. 

2. The variance of each term is V(xi/n) = σ²/n². 

3. For independent variables the variances add: V(x̄) = n · (σ²/n²). 

4. Hence **V(x̄) = σ² / n** . 

**V( x̄ ) = σ² / n** 

**Meaning.** The sampling distribution of the mean is _narrower_ than the population distribution. The larger the sample size n, the smaller the variability of x̄. Intuition: ten large samples will have means very close to each other, whereas ten very small samples may have very different means. 

###### **2.4 Normal parent population** 

If the population is normal with mean μ and variance σ², then the sampling distribution of the mean is also **normal** with mean μ and variance σ²/n — even for tiny samples (size 3 or 4). An important assumption is that the random variables in the sample are **independent** . 

###### **2.5 Central Limit Theorem (CLT)** 

What if the population distribution is not normal, or is unknown? Take samples of large size (for example 35). The distribution of the sample means **tends toward normality** purely because the sample size is large. This is the **Central Limit Theorem** . It is very useful because we get a nearly normal distribution without knowing the population distribution. 

**Large n (say n > 30): x̄ is approximately Normal( mean = μ, variance = σ² / n )** 

— 5 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **Comparison: normal vs non-normal parent population** 

|**Situation**|**Population**|**Sample size needed**|**Sampling distribution of x̄**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Normal parent|Normal (μ, σ²)|Any n (even 3 or 4)|Normal, mean μ, variance σ²/n|
|Non-normal / unknown|Not normal or unknown|Large (say > 30)|Approximately normal, mean μ,|
|parent|||variance σ²/n (CLT)|



###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Gaussian** is another name for the **normal** distribution. 

- **Central Limit Theorem:** even if the population is not normal, the distribution of the sample mean tends towards normality provided the sample size is large (say > 30). 

- The sampling distribution of the mean has the **same mean μ** as the population but a **smaller variance σ²/n** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- E(x̄) = μ but V(x̄) = σ²/n — **not** σ². 

- Increasing sample size **reduces** the variance of x̄ (inverse relation with n). 

- Normal parent → sampling distribution is normal for **any** sample size. Non-normal parent → normal only **approximately and for large n** . 

- The derivation assumes the observations are **independent** . 

- Sample size “greater than 30” is the rule of thumb quoted for the CLT. 

— 6 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 3 · Confidence Intervals** 

_Covers: Lecture 34 (middle part)_ 

###### **3.1 Idea** 

A point estimate (x̄) is one number. A **confidence interval** wraps a range around it, built so that the range is expected to contain the true population mean μ most of the time. 

###### **3.2 The formula (σ known, normal population)** 

If x̄ is the mean of a random sample of size n from a **normal** population with **known variance σ²** , then the **100(1 − α)%** confidence interval on μ is: 

**x̄ − zα/2 · σ/√n ≤ μ ≤ x̄ + zα/2 · σ/√n** 

Symbols: x̄ = sample mean · σ = population standard deviation (known) · n = sample size · α = level of significance · zα/2 = a value read from the standard normal table. 

###### **3.3 What are α and zα/2?** 

Any normal variable x can be converted to a **standard normal** variable Z = (x − μ)/σ, which has mean 0 and standard deviation 1. The curve is symmetric. Choose two points, +zα/2 and −zα/2, equally far from 0, such that the area in each tail beyond them is α/2. So zα/2 is the **upper 100·α/2 percentage point** of the standard normal distribution. The total tail area is **α** , called the **level of significance** . 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
Standard normal curve<br>mean 0, SD 1, symmetric<br>1 − α α = 0.05 → each tail 0.025<br>(confidence level) → 95 % confidence interval<br>α/2 α/2<br>−z(α/2) 0 +z(α/2)<br><!-- End of picture text -->

###### **3.4 Step-by-step: building a 95 % confidence interval** 

1. Choose α = 0.05 (the commonly used value) → confidence level = 1 − α = 0.95 = 95 %. 

2. Tail area on each side = α/2 = 0.025. 

3. From the standard normal table find zα/2 such that the area beyond it is 0.025. 

4. Compute the half-width zα/2 · σ/√n (a constant for given n and σ). 

5. Lower limit = x̄ − half-width; upper limit = x̄ + half-width. 

###### **3.5 Correct interpretation** 

Different samples give different x̄ values, so the interval shifts from sample to sample (the half-width stays the same). If you take 100 random samples and build a 95 % interval from each, then about **95 of those 100 intervals** are expected to bracket (contain) the true population mean μ. 

###### **3.6 Precision vs confidence** 

Back to the train: “between 1 and 5” is almost certainly right but is too vague to be useful; “between 2:00 and 2:15” is precise but you cannot be very confident, because the train might have come at 1:55. 

— 7 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **Comparison: broad vs narrow confidence interval** 

|**Interval type**|**Width**|**Precision**|**Confidence attached**|
|---|---|---|---|
|Broad interval (e.g., train between 1 and 5)|Wide|Less precise (vague)|High|
|Narrow interval (e.g., train between 2 and<br>2:15)|Narrow|More precise|Low|



###### **FORMULAS, SYMBOLS & CONSTANTS** 

- 100(1 − α)% CI for μ: x̄ ± zα/2 · σ/√n (σ known, normal population) 

- α = level of significance; usual value **0.05** → 95 % CI; each tail = **0.025** . 

- Z = (x − μ)/σ → standard normal: mean 0, SD 1. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- The confidence interval is defined on the **population parameter μ** , using the sample mean x̄. 

- The formula above applies when **σ is known** and the population is **normal** . If σ is unknown and n is small → use the t-distribution (Chapter 4). 

- “95 % confidence” = 95 out of 100 such intervals are expected to bracket μ — not a statement about a single sample’s mean. 

- zα/2 uses **α/2** (two tails), while the chi-square and F cut-off values use the full **α** in one tail. 

- Wider interval → more confidence but less precision; narrower interval → more precision but less confidence. 

— 8 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 4 · The t, Chi-Square (χ²) and F Distributions** 

_Covers: Lecture 34 (t, χ²) and Lecture 32 (F)_ 

Along with the normal distribution, three more sampling distributions are used constantly in hypothesis testing, regression and Design of Experiments. 

###### **4.1 t-distribution** 

**When?** When the **sample size is small** and the **population variance σ² is unknown** (which is usually the case). The only assumption is that the population is normal — “not a very serious assumption”, since many populations tend towards normality. Instead of σ, we use the **sample standard deviation s** . 

**t = ( x̄ − μ ) / ( s / √n ) with n − 1 degrees of freedom** 

The t-distribution is often used in hypothesis testing, linear regression and design of experiments. Degrees of freedom are n − 1 because s itself has n − 1 independent deviations. 

###### **4.2 Chi-square (χ²) distribution** 

**When?** To report confidence intervals on the **population variance** (just as z is used for the mean). Assumption: the population is normal. 

**χ² = ( n − 1 ) · s² / σ² with n − 1 degrees of freedom** 

The chi-square curve starts at **zero** and has **only positive values** , and only **one tail** (the right tail) is used. The cut-off χ²α,k is defined so that the area to its right is α: 

**P ( χ² > χ²α,k ) = α (χ²α,k = upper 100α percent point, k degrees of freedom)** 

###### **4.3 F distribution** 

**Why do we need it?** The variability in an experiment has two possible sources: (a) changing the factor levels, and (b) random noise you cannot control. To claim a factor really matters, its variability must be **much larger** than the noise variability. If the two are comparable, the factor is not making a real impact. The F distribution is the tool for comparing two variances (taking their ratio). 

**Assumptions:** the two populations being compared are both **normally distributed** , and their means and variances are **unknown** . 

**Definition:** F is the ratio of two **independent** chi-square variables, each divided by its own degrees of freedom (m1 and m2). 

**F = ( χ²1 / m1 ) / ( χ²2 / m2 ) = ( S1² / σ1² ) / ( S2² / σ2² )** 

The (m − 1) factors in χ² = (m − 1)S²/σ² cancel with the division by the df, giving the simple ratio on the right. S1², S2² are the sample variances; σ1², σ2² the population variances. 

**Two degrees of freedom.** Unlike z and χ², F has **two** df: the **numerator df** (m − 1) and the **denominator df** (n − 1), always quoted in that order (numerator first). The percentage point fα,m1,m2 is defined by P(F > fα,m1,m2) = α, i.e., the area under the F curve from f to infinity is α. 

**Numerical value to remember:** with numerator df = 10, denominator df = 10 and α = 0.05, f0.05,10,10 = **2.98** — the area beyond 2.98 is 0.05. 

— 9 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **Comparison table: the four distributions** 

|**Feature**|**Normal (Z)**|**t**|**Chi-square (χ²)**|**F**|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|Main use|CI on mean (σ<br>known)|Mean when σ<br>unknown, small n|CI / tests on a<br>variance|Compare two<br>variances; ANOVA|
|Formula|(x − μ)/σ|(x̄ − μ)/(s/√n)|(n − 1)s²/σ²|(S₁²/σ₁²)/(S₂²/σ₂²)|
|df|—|n − 1|n − 1|Two: numerator,<br>denominator|
|Shape / tails|Symmetric, two<br>tails (α/2 each)|Symmetric<br>(bell-like)|Only positive values,<br>one (right) tail|Only positive, one<br>(right) tail|
|Key assumption|Normal population|Normal population|Normal population|Both populations<br>normal|
|Direct use in<br>Design of<br>Experiments?|—|Yes (regression,<br>DoE)|Not immediately|**Yes — directly**|



###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- t is used when **n is small AND σ is unknown** ; it has **n − 1** df and uses **s** in place of σ. 

- χ² takes **only positive values** ; its cut-off uses **one tail** (area α to the right). 

- F is a **ratio of two variances** ; it has **two** degrees of freedom — quote numerator first. 

- The chi-square distribution is “not really immediately used” in DoE, but the **F distribution finds direct application** . 

- f0.05,10,10 = 2.98. 

— 10 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 5 · Hypothesis Testing** 

_Covers: Lecture 32 (first part) and Lecture 34 (last part)_ 

###### **5.1 The idea in plain words** 

You make a **claim** (a hypothesis) about a **population** , then use the data in a **sample** to judge whether the claim is adequately supported. Important: hypothesis testing is about **parameters of the population distribution** , not about the sample itself. The sample is only the evidence used to decide about the population. 

###### **5.2 Null and alternate hypotheses** 

There are always two hypotheses. The **null hypothesis (H0)** is the original, “nothing special” statement. The **alternate hypothesis (H1)** is its nullification, i.e., the contradiction. If H0 says “something is working”, H1 says “it is not working”; if H0 says “the person is innocent”, H1 says “the person is not innocent”. Rejection of H0 means **automatic acceptance** of H1. 

###### **5.3 Procedure** 

1. State H0 and H1 about a population parameter. 

2. Take a sample and choose a **test statistic** — a function of the sample measurements. The two seen so far are the **sample mean** (to infer about the population mean) and the **sample variance** (to infer about the population variance). 

3. Use the test statistic to try to establish H0 or H1. 

4. Make a decision: **reject H0** or **do not reject H0** . 

5. Remember that every decision may be wrong; know the two possible errors. 

###### **5.4 Decisions and errors**
