Note the careful wording: we say “ **do not reject H0** ” and avoid saying “accept H0”. Rejection, however, is unambiguous. 

###### **Decision table** 

|**Statistical decision**|**H0 is actually TRUE**|**H0 is actually FALSE**|
|---|---|---|
|Do not reject H0|Correct decision|**Type II error**(less serious)|
|Reject H0|**Type I error**(more serious)|Correct decision|



###### **Comparison: Type I vs Type II error** 

||**Type I error**|**Type II error**|
|---|---|---|
|What happens|H0is true but is rejected|H0is false but is not rejected|
|Seriousness|**More serious**|**Less serious**|
|Court example (H0:|Innocent person convicted and punished|Guilty person set free|
|accused is innocent)|||
|Industry example (H0:|A good existing process is thrown away;|A better process is missed, but you are|
|new process is no|time, money and manpower invested in a|only back where you started — plant|
|improvement)|new process that gives no real benefit|keeps running and profits continue|



###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

— 11 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- **Industrial process.** A well-established process has run profitably for 20 years. A new recruit proposes an improvement. H0: the proposed process is _not_ a significant improvement. H1: the proposed process _is_ a significant improvement. 

- **Court.** H0: the accused is not guilty. H1: the accused is guilty. Principle: many guilty may escape, but not a single innocent person should be wrongly punished — hence Type I is the more severe error. 

- **In Design of Experiments.** Several variables may influence a process. Start by assuming (H0) that _none_ of the factors is significant; then, from the experimental data, conclude that some are significant and the rest are not. 

###### **5.5 The p-value (as introduced in the course)** 

The **p-value** refers to the probability of committing the **Type I error** . When the F value is very high, you are far out in the tail of the F distribution, so the p-value is very small — i.e., the chance of a Type I error is small and the factor is judged significant. 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Hypothesis testing** concerns the parameters of the **population** , using information from the sample. 

- **Null hypothesis** H0 = original claim (“no effect / not working / innocent”). **Alternate** H1 = its nullification. 

- **Test statistic** = a function of the sample measurements (e.g., sample mean, sample variance). 

- **Type I error** = rejecting a true H0. **Type II error** = not rejecting a false H0. 

- **p-value** ↔ probability of committing a Type I error; very high F → very small p. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Say “ **do not reject** H0”, not “accept H0”. 

- Rejecting H0 ⇒ H1 is accepted automatically. 

- The more serious error is **Type I** ; the less serious is **Type II** . 

- Decisions are made about the **population** , never about the sample. 

- Decision making is **always** associated with the possibility of error. 

- In DoE the usual starting H0 is that **no factor is significant** . 

— 12 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 6 · Experimental Variability, One-Factor ANOVA and Randomization** 

_Covers: Lecture 32 (second half)_ 

###### **6.1 Single-factor experiments and “treatments”** 

The simplest experiments study **one factor** at several **levels** . In statistics these levels are called **treatments** — a classical term that has simply survived over the years; it does _not_ mean a medical treatment. Example: fertilizer A and fertilizer B applied to land are two treatments of one factor (fertilizer). 

###### **6.2 Where does variability come from?** 

If you repeat an experiment with every factor held at the same values, any variation in the results is due only to **random factors** (assuming instruments are working properly and there are no systematic errors). Hence **repeats** are used to measure the experimental error — the more repeats, the better your idea of the error. 

The central question of a one-factor experiment: is the **variation between treatments** large compared with the **variation within treatments** (the variation due to repeats)? If yes, the factor has a real effect; if they are comparable, it does not. 

###### **6.3 The ANOVA table (Analysis of Variance)** 

**ANOVA** = Analysis Of VAriance: everything boils down to analysing variability. Let **a** = number of levels (treatments) and **n** = number of repeats per level. 

1. **Sum of squares.** Compute the sum of squares of treatments (SST) and of error (SSE). We square deviations because, relative to an average, some are positive and some negative. 

2. **Degrees of freedom.** Treatments: **a − 1** . Error: **a(n − 1)** . 

3. **Mean squares.** Divide each sum of squares by its df, to put them on the same basis (sample sizes differ): MST = SST/(a − 1); MSE = SSE/[a(n − 1)]. A mean square is a kind of variance. 

4. **F value.** F = MST / MSE. 

5. **Decide.** Use the probability (p) associated with F: a large F means the treatment effect dominates over error. 

###### **One-factor ANOVA table** 

|**Source of variation**|**Sum of**|**Degrees of**|**Mean square**|**F**|
|---|---|---|---|---|
||**squares**|**freedom**|||
|Treatments|SST|a − 1|MST= SST/(a − 1)|F = MST/ MSE|
|Error|SSE|a(n − 1)|MSE= SSE/[a(n − 1)]||



###### **6.4 Randomization of runs** 

A new experimenter tends to vary settings very systematically (low → high for one variable, then the next variable). This looks organised but is risky. It is better to run the experiments in a **randomized order** . 

###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

- **Photocopier toner.** Compare 3 toners (A, B, C) with 3 copies each by judging picture quality. 

- **Systematic order:** A, A, A, then B, B, B, then C, C, C. The photocopier heats up with time, so toner C suffers poor quality because of heating, not because of the toner → the wrong conclusion “toner C is bad”. 

— 13 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- **Randomized order:** e.g., 1, 2, 3, 2, 3, 1, 1, 3, 2. Now the heating effect (inevitable and not controllable) touches all three toners and is spread evenly across all factor settings. 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **Treatment** = a level of the factor (classical term). 

- **ANOVA** = analysis of variance; compares variability due to treatments with variability due to error. 

- **Randomization** = running the designed experiments in random order **and** allocating experimental material to runs randomly, so that unwanted effects are spread evenly over all factor settings. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- df for treatments = **a − 1** ; df for error = **a(n − 1)** . 

- F = **MStreatment / MSerror** (mean squares, not raw sums of squares — scale by df first). 

- Repeats estimate **random error** ; this assumes **no systematic error** (instruments working properly). 

- If treatment variability is **comparable** to error variability → the factor is **not** making a real impact. 

- Randomization is about **order of runs** and **allocation of material** ; its purpose is to spread uncontrollable effects evenly. 

— 14 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 7 · Factorial Design, Interaction and Two-Factor ANOVA** 

_Covers: Lecture 32 (last part) and Lecture 33 (first half)_ 

###### **7.1 2ⁿ factorial design** 

In a **2**<sup>**n**</sup> **factorial design** , **2** is the number of **levels** of each factor and **n** is the number of **factors** . Every combination of levels is run. 

**Example.** Three factors — temperature, pressure and concentration — each at a higher and a lower level: 2<sup>3</sup> = **8** experiments. 

###### **7.2 Advantages of factorial design** 

- Results can be interpreted scientifically; the design economizes the experimental programme yet gives valuable information, even with only two levels. 

- It enables optimization approaches such as **Response Surface Methodology** . 

- **Qualitative and quantitative factors** can be analysed together — e.g., three catalyst types (discrete entities) together with temperature or pressure (continuously varying). 

- Described as compulsory for **industrial competitiveness** . 

- Caution: 2<sup>n</sup> still becomes large when the number of factors is large. 

###### **7.3 One-factor-at-a-time (OFAT) vs factorial** 

###### **Comparison table** 

|**Aspect**|**One factor at a time**|**Factorial design**|
|---|---|---|
|Number of runs|More runs|Limited number of runs for maximum<br>information|
|Interactions between factors|Not detected|Detected|
|Verdict|Not a good strategy|Recommended|



###### **7.4 What is an interaction?** 

Two factors **interact** when the effect of changing one factor depends on the level at which the other factor is held. Formal statement: if a change in the level of the first factor causes a certain change in response at one level of the second factor, but an identical change at the second level of the second factor produces a markedly different response, then the factors interact. 

###### **7.5 Worked case: the cricket experiment (two factors, interaction)** 

###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

- **Question.** Does the type of drink taken before batting (beer or tea) and the weight of the bat (light or heavy) influence runs scored? Response = runs scored; each batsman has two innings per match. Analysis done in the statistical software **Minitab** (which also supports design of experiments). 

- **Cell averages:** tea + heavy bat = **24** runs; beer + heavy bat = **51** runs (the highest). 

- **Main-effects plot** (each factor alone): going from light to heavy bat appears to improve performance, and going from beer to tea appears to improve performance — but this is **not the complete picture** . 

— 15 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- **Interaction plot:** with a light bat, going from beer to tea increases performance; with a heavy bat, beer + heavy bat performs **better** than tea + heavy bat. The lines are not parallel ⇒ **strong interaction** between drink and bat weight. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
NO interaction: parallel lines<br><!-- End of picture text -->



<!-- Start of picture text -->
B low<br>B high<br>low level of A high level of A<br><!-- End of picture text -->



<!-- Start of picture text -->
INTERACTION: lines cross/converge/diverge<br>B low<br>B high<br>low level of A high level of A<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**Rule:** if the lines in an interaction plot are **parallel** , the variables do **not** interact. If they **intersect, approach one another or diverge** , there is an interaction. Hence do not look only at single-factor (main-effect) plots — always look at interaction plots. 

###### **7.6 Two-factor ANOVA table** 

Now there are two factors (A with **a** levels, B with **b** levels) and also their interaction AB. Each sum of squares is scaled by its degrees of freedom to get mean squares, and each is compared with the error mean square (from repeats — either all runs repeated, or repeats at the centre point). 

###### **Two-factor ANOVA table** 

|**Source of variation**|**df**|**Mean square**|**F**|
|---|---|---|---|
|A treatments|a − 1|SSA/(a − 1)|MSA/MSE|
|B treatments|b − 1|SSB/(b − 1)|MSB/MSE|
|Interaction AB|(a − 1)(b − 1)|SSAB/[(a − 1)(b − 1)]|MSAB/MSE|
|Error (from repeats)|—|MSE||



**Reading F values:** if a treatment is very important, its mean square **dominates** over the error mean square and F is very high. If the interaction is not important, MSAB is comparable to MSE, so F is of the order of 1 → the term is not important. A very high F gives a very small p-value (tail of the F distribution). 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **2**<sup>**n**</sup> **factorial design:** 2 levels, n factors → 2<sup>n</sup> runs (e.g., 3 factors → 8 runs). 

- **Interaction:** the effect of one factor depends on the level of another. 

- **Parallel lines** in an interaction plot ⇒ no interaction; **crossing / converging / diverging** ⇒ interaction. 

- Factorial design handles **both qualitative (discrete) and quantitative (continuous)** factors together. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- A main-effects plot alone can mislead; always check the **interaction plot** . 

- df of the interaction = **(a − 1)(b − 1)** , the **product** of the individual df; F ≈ 1 ⇒ source not important, very high F ⇒ dominant effect. 

- Cricket example numbers: **24** (tea + heavy) and **51** (beer + heavy, highest); lines cross ⇒ strong interaction. 

— 16 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 8 · Empirical Regression Models** 

_Covers: Lecture 33 (second half)_ 

###### **8.1 Purpose** 

Once experiments are done and analysed, the data can be used to build a simple mathematical model — an **empirical polynomial regression model** . It is not derived from first principles and has little theoretical background, but it provides a useful formula to **predict the response at different factor settings** . 

###### **8.2 The simple linear model** 

**y = β0 + β1x1 + β2x2 + ε** 

- **y** = process response; **x1, x2** = the variables (factors, also called regressors). 

- **β0** = intercept (stands on its own, like b in y = mx + b). 

- **β1, β2** = **partial regression coefficients** of the variables. 

- **ε** = random error: assumed to vary randomly with **mean 0 and constant variance σ²** . Therefore the response is normally distributed about the true line with variance σ². 

- The **true** line (true β values) is unknown; data scatter around it. Regression only **estimates** the parameters. 

- Each repeat of the experiment gives different responses and hence slightly different estimates — this is **expected** , not a worry. 

###### **8.3 Multiple regression with k regressors** 

With k regressor variables: yi = β0 + β1xi1 + β2xi2 + … + βkxik + εi, i = 1 … n. The first subscript **i** is the **run order** (1 to n); the second subscript **j** indexes the regressor. For instance xi2 = setting of the 2nd variable in the i-th run. 

###### **Number of parameters p = k + 1 (k partial regression coefficients + 1 intercept)** 

- Number of runs must satisfy **n > k** ; if n < k there is not enough data. Even n = k is insufficient. 

- At least **k + 1** runs are needed. With exactly k + 1 runs the model fits the data **perfectly** (k + 1 equations in k + 1 unknowns) — but this is not good, because it means too many terms and an unwieldy model. 

- A good regression model is **simple** and explains the process with few parameters. 

- It is **empirical** : valid only within the narrow range of the variables used; **do not extrapolate** beyond those ranges. 

###### **8.4 Matrix form and parameter estimation** 

Matrix notation gives compact forms. Linear algebra needed: adding, subtracting, multiplying and representing matrices, plus brief ideas on eigenvalues and eigenvectors. 

— 17 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **Notation for the matrix model** 

|**Symbol**|**Meaning**|**Shape / content**|
|---|---|---|
|Y|Vector of observed responses|n × 1 : Y1, Y2, …, Yn|
|X|Design matrix|n × (k + 1): first column all**1’s**(for the intercept); then x11…<br>x1kfor run 1, … up to xn1… xnk|
|β|Vector of parameters|(k + 1) × 1 : β0, β1, …, βk|
|ε|Vector of errors|n × 1 : ε1, …, εn|
|X′|Transpose of X|Interchange rows and columns of X|
|||**Y = Xβ + ε**|



**Hat notation.** Because the true β is unknown, we work with estimated quantities: **ŷ = β̂0 + β̂1x1 + … + β̂kxk** . The hat (^) means _predicted_ response or _estimated_ parameter — not the experimental response or the true parameter. 

###### **Steps to estimate β̂:** 

1. Form the X matrix (column of ones + factor settings). 

2. Form its transpose X′ by interchanging rows and columns. 

3. Compute X′X and X′Y. 

4. Solve: β̂ = (X′X)<sup>−1</sup> X′Y. 

5. Software such as **MATLAB** or **Scilab** does the matrix inversion and multiplication quickly (or write your own program). 

**β̂ = ( X′X )**<sup>**−1**</sup> **X′Y** 

###### **8.5 Sums of squares and the regression ANOVA** 

To judge which model parameters really matter (and which are “dud” parameters), compute the error sum of squares: 

###### **SSE = Y′Y − β̂′X′Y i.e. Total SS − Regression SS = Error SS** 

Y′Y is the **total sum of squares** and β̂′X′Y is the **regression sum of squares** . Ideally they would be equal, but total SS usually exceeds regression SS; the larger the gap, the larger the contribution of random error. 

###### **Regression ANOVA table** 

|**Source**|**Sum of squares**|**df**|**Mean square**|**F**|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|Regression|SSR|k|MSR= SSR/k|F = MSR/MSE|
|Error|SSE|n − k − 1|MSE= SSE/(n − k − 1)||
|Total|SSR+ SSE|n − 1|||



###### **8.6 R² (coefficient of determination) and adjusted R²** 

**R²** is the proportion of the total variability **accounted for (explained) by the regression model** . R² = 1 would mean all variability is explained. But a high R² can be cheaply obtained: 

- If the **number of parameters equals the number of runs** , you are just solving linear algebra, not doing real regression, and R² is always 1. 

— 18 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- Adding more and more useless terms (e.g., β11x1², β22x2²) pushes R² toward 1. Such terms are still “regression” because the model remains **linear in the coefficients** ; you just add columns (x1², x2²) to X. 

- A model with R² = **0.84** may be more robust, informative and reliable than one with **15 parameters and R² = 1** , which can be unstable and very sensitive. 

**Adjusted R²** penalizes the use of too many parameters: 

###### **R²adj = 1 − [ SSE/(n − p) ] / [ SST/(n − 1) ] = 1 − MSE / MST (p = k + 1)** 

When parameters are added, p rises so (n − p) falls, which tends to **increase** the MSE term and **reduce** R²adj. R²adj goes up only if SSE falls faster than (n − p) falls — i.e., only when the new parameter genuinely improves prediction. 

###### **Comparison: R² vs adjusted R²** 

||**R²**|**Adjusted R²**|
|---|---|---|
|Meaning|Fraction of total variability explained|R² corrected for the number of<br>parameters|
|Effect of adding more<br>parameters|Keeps rising (tends to 1)|May**decrease**if the parameter is not<br>useful|
|Use|Do not chase unrealistically high values|If it decreases on adding parameters →<br>**do not add them**; keep the simpler<br>model|



###### **FORMULAS, SYMBOLS & CONSTANTS** 

- Model: y = β0 + Σβjxj + ε ; parameters p = **k + 1** 

- Estimate: **β̂ = (X′X)**<sup>**−1**</sup> **X′Y** 

- SSE = Y′Y − β̂′X′Y ; SST = SSR + SSE 

- df: regression = **k** ; error = **n − k − 1** ; total = **n − 1** 

- F = MSR/MSE; MSR = SSR/k; MSE = SSE/(n − k − 1) 

- R²adj = 1 − [SSE/(n − p)] / [SST/(n − 1)] 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- **k** regressors → **k + 1** parameters (the extra one is the intercept β0). 

- Need **n > k** ; minimum useful condition is at least k + 1 runs; n = k + 1 gives an exact (but undesirable) fit with R² = 1. 

- Hat (^) = **predicted/estimated** ; no hat = true or experimental value. 

- Errors assumed: **mean 0, constant variance σ²** , response normally distributed about the true line. 

- Regression models are **empirical** : never extrapolate outside the range of the factors. 

- Different experiments give different parameter estimates — **expected** . 

- A very high R² is **not** automatically good; check R²adj. 

- Adding x² terms keeps the model a **linear regression** (linear in the β’s). 

— 19 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 9 · Second-Order Models and the Central Composite Design (CCD)** 

_Covers: Lecture 33 (end) and Lecture 31 (beginning of the file; labelled “Lecture – 05”)_ 

###### **9.1 Why second-order models?** 

When the response is plotted against the factors, the experimental “design space” is usually **not a flat plane** ; it has **peaks (mountains) and valleys (depressions)** . A first-order (planar) model cannot capture these. A **second-order model** is needed to describe curvature, and therefore to find the best optimum. Sometimes the optimum is a **maximum** , sometimes a **minimum** . There may be **multiple peaks and valleys** , so one should not be satisfied with a sub-optimal solution. 

###### **9.2 Form of the second-order model** 

###### **y = β0 + Σ βixi + Σi<j βijxixj + Σ βiixi² + ε** 

###### **Terms of the second-order model** 

|**Group of terms**|**Name**|**Two-factor example**|
|---|---|---|
|Σ βixi|Linear (main-effect) terms — single factors|β1x1+ β2x2|
|Σ βijxixj|Interaction terms — all possible factor pairs|β12x1x2|
|Σ βiixi²|Quadratic (pure second-order) terms —<br>responsible for curvature|β11x1² + β22x2²|



For two factors the full model is y = β0 + β1x1 + β2x2 + β12x1x2 + β11x1² + β22x2² + ε — i.e., **6 parameters** to estimate (β0, β1, β2, β12, β11, β22). _(Counting terms in the general formula for k factors gives (k + 1)(k + 2)/2 — this count follows directly from the model structure above.)_ 

###### **9.3 Central Composite Design — structure** 

The CCD is a very popular second-order experimental design. Take the case of **2 factors** : 

1. **Factorial part (the square):** a regular 2<sup>2</sup> design — 2 factors at 2 levels = 4 settings (low–low, high–high, etc.). 

2. **Axial points:** 2k points, where k = number of factors → 4 axial points for k = 2. (Their numbering is arbitrary.) 

3. So far 4 + 4 = **8** runs. 

4. **Centre point:** the geometric centre of the design. Repeats may be performed at the factorial points or at the centre point; here **4 or 5 repeats** at the centre are typical. 

— 20 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
x2<br>4 factorial points = 2² (square corners)<br>4 axial points = 2k (k = 2 factors)<br>centre point (repeated 4–5 times)<br>x1 Total = 4 + 4 + centre repeats<br><!-- End of picture text -->

_Central Composite Design for two factors (k = 2)_ 

**Flexibility.** The axial points can be placed farther from, or closer to, the factorial points depending on requirements (learned in a formal Design of Experiments course). 

###### **9.4 Why centre runs? What do axial runs add?** 

###### **Roles of each part of the CCD** 

|**Component**|**Contribution**|
|---|---|
|Centre-point runs|Serve as**repeats**cheaply (repeating every factorial point becomes very<br>expensive when factors are many). The centre represents the overall design; it is<br>an important**augmentation of the factorial design**; and it tells whether<br>there is**curvature**in the response.|
|What centre points can tell|Whether the**sum**β11+ β22is significant. They**cannot**estimate β11and β22<br>individually. If the sum is insignificant, both quadratic terms need not be in the<br>model.|
|Axial points|Allow the significance of the**individual pure quadratic effects**to be<br>estimated. Without axial points only the sum of the quadratic terms could be<br>judged (from centre points).|
|What axial points do NOT do|They do**not**contribute to estimating the**interaction**effects.|
|Centre + axial points<br>together|Contribute to the**flexibility**of the CCD.|



**Linear vs curved response.** If factors only act linearly, the response is a plane. When factors interact, you get a curvature (a “twist”) in the plane — centre points are needed to detect this. (How exactly they detect curvature is beyond the scope of the introduction.) 

###### **KEY DEFINITIONS & ONE-LINERS** 

- **CCD (k factors)** = 2<sup>k</sup> factorial points + 2k axial points + centre point(s). 

- For **k = 2** : 4 factorial + 4 axial = 8 runs, plus **4–5 centre repeats** . 

- **Centre points → detect curvature** ; **axial points → individual quadratic terms** . 

- The centre point is the **geometric centre** of the design. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Centre points test only whether **β11 + β22** (sum) is significant — **not** each one separately. 

- Axial points estimate **individual** quadratic effects but **not interactions** . 

- Number of axial points = **2k** (k = number of factors). Second-order models capture **peaks and valleys** ; optimum = maximum **or** minimum. 

— 21 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 10 · Response Surface Methodology (RSM)** 

_Covers: Lecture 31 (second half) — closing topic of the Design of Experiments series_ 

###### **10.1 The problem RSM solves** 

Many industries want to optimize their processes but do not know where to start or end. It is not appropriate to wander through an n-dimensional space of factors hoping to land on the optimum. The current operating level is usually **far from the optimum** . 

**Approach.** First run a set of **screening experiments** (a preliminary set) to assess the overall trend. Then **Response Surface Methodology** identifies the **direction** in which to set the experimental factors so that the response improves in the fastest manner, helping you plan the next level of experiments. 

Typical objectives (to be maximised or minimised): reaction yield, conversion, process time, energy consumed. The objective function may be a maximum _or_ a minimum, so go in the correct desired direction. 

For two variables x1 and x2, the **method of steepest ascent** is the optimisation tool that gives the direction in which the response increases fastest (shown as an arrow on the contour plot). 

###### **10.2 Finding the stationary (optimum) point — in ordinary algebra** 

1. Fit the second-order model: ŷ = β̂0 + β̂1x1 + β̂2x2 + β̂12x1x2 + β̂11x1² + β̂22x2². 

2. Partially differentiate the model with respect to x1 and with respect to x2. 

3. Set each derivative equal to **zero** . 

4. Solve the resulting system of equations → the **stationary point(s)** . 

With many factors this is tedious, so linear-algebra tools (MATLAB, Scilab, or your own program) are used. 

###### **10.3 The same thing in matrix notation** 

**ŷ = β̂0 + x′b + x′Bx** 

- **x** = vector of the factors x1, …, xk. 

- **b** = vector of the first-order estimated coefficients β̂1 … β̂k. 

- **B** = matrix with the pure quadratic coefficients β̂ii on the diagonal and the interaction coefficients β̂ij **divided by 2** in the off-diagonal positions. 

- Bold symbols denote vectors/matrices, not ordinary scalars. 

Differentiate in matrix terms and equate to zero: 

**∂ŷ/∂x = b + 2Bx = 0 ⇒ xs = − ½ · B**<sup>**−1**</sup> **· b** 

Substituting xs back into the model gives the predicted response at the stationary point. 

###### **10.4 Is it a maximum or a minimum? — eigenvalues of B** 

Find the **eigenvalues of the matrix B** : 

— 22 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **Classifying the stationary point** 

|**Eigenvalues of B**|**Stationary point is a …**|
|---|---|
|**All positive**|**Minimum**|
|**All negative**|**Maximum**|
|One positive and one negative (mixed signs)|A complicated case — not covered in the course|



###### **10.5 Prerequisites highlighted at the end of the series** 

To understand, analyse and present results of designed experiments, a proper basis in statistics and probability is needed: the **normal distribution** , the **random variable** , **sampling distributions of the mean** , the **chi-square** distribution, the **F distribution** and **hypothesis-testing** concepts (Chapters 1–5 of this guide). 

###### **FORMULAS, SYMBOLS & CONSTANTS** 

- Matrix second-order model: **ŷ = β̂0 + x′b + x′Bx** 

- Stationary condition: **b + 2Bx = 0** 

- Stationary point: **xs = −½ B**<sup>**−1**</sup> **b** 

- Eigenvalues of B: **all +ve → minimum** ; **all −ve → maximum** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- RSM is preceded by **screening experiments** (preliminary experiments to see the trend). 

- • The **method of steepest ascent** shows the direction in which response increases **fastest** . • Stationary point: derivative = 0, i.e., **xs = −½ B**<sup>**−1**</sup> **b** (note the **minus ½** ). 

- **Positive** eigenvalues → **minimum** ; **negative** eigenvalues → **maximum** (easy to reverse by mistake). 

- In matrix B, the interaction coefficients appear **divided by 2** . 

- The objective may be to **maximise** (yield, conversion) or **minimise** (process time, energy consumed). 

— 23 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 11 · Research in Applied Mechanics** 

_Covers: Lecture 35 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **11.1 Structure and traditional areas** 

The department is organised into **three groups** . Faculty come from **8 different undergraduate backgrounds** — from physicists to aerospace, mechanical and civil engineers — giving a “melting pot” of sciences and engineering and a strongly interdisciplinary flavour. Applied Mechanics is a **postgraduate-only** department (and IIT as a whole is a predominantly PG institution). 

###### **The three research groups** 

|**Group**|**Focus**|
|---|---|
|Fluid Mechanics|Problems of fluid mechanics|
|Solid Mechanics|Problems of solid mechanics|
|Bio Mechanics & Bio-Medical Engineering|Biomechanics and biomedical engineering problems|



Classically the department focused on **mechanics problems in fluids and solids** . More recently, faculty have moved into **fluid–structure interaction** , **cell mechanics** (biomechanics interfacing with solid mechanics) and **interfacing electrical engineering into mechanical engineering** . 

###### **11.2 Newer areas of research and what enabled them** 

Two technological advances have enabled many newer research areas worldwide: 

1. Extremely **fast computational tools** . 

2. Extremely **sophisticated analysis and visualization tools** such as electron microscopy. 

###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

- **Experiments:** studying tissue properties using **nano-indentation** and **atomic force microscopy** . 

- **Computations:** very high-fidelity computations of industry-scale **combustion** problems using **direct numerical simulation** of a particular kind of flow. 

- These are still **emerging** areas; the expectation is that in a few years such tools will solve industry-scale problems. 

**India-specific problems.** Using traditionally developed research tools, many Indian problems can be solved — for organizations such as **DRDO** , **ISRO** , public sector undertakings and **rural problems** . These do not need extremely sophisticated tools but need “India-level, India-scale” thinking; traditional solutions are not discarded because they are ready to solve Indian problems. 

###### **11.3 Challenges for incoming MS / PhD scholars** 

###### **Classroom vs research** 

||**Classroom problem**|**Research problem**|
|---|---|---|
|Nature|Well-defined|Open-ended|
|Example (equation with 4<br>variables)|Given 3 variables, find the 4th|Given only 1 variable; you must work out<br>the other 3 and**justify your own**<br>**assumptions**|



- A second challenge: **defining your own area of research** . Faculty often allow scholars freedom to choose, within the limits of what the faculty are interested in and equipped to work on. 

- Both are called **positive challenges** where scholars grow. 

— 24 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- **Approach:** be open-minded, grow into the solution, use available resources and the **diversified peer group** (students come from many different backgrounds). 

###### **11.4 Industry connection** 

As a **publicly funded institution** , serving Indian industry is part of the mandate (of faculty and scholars). Direct links: faculty serve on expert committees and run projects with **DRDO** and **ISRO** . Indirect link: working on industry-scale problems trains scholars for later absorption — graduates have been absorbed by companies such as **GE, Boeing and Mercedes Benz** . 

###### **11.5 Measuring success in research** 

Growth over the years shows mainly as (i) a big increase in **confidence** in one’s own abilities, and (ii) a wider **skill set** from sampling many techniques in a world-class infrastructure and peer environment (confidence grows most in the last ~2 years of a typical PhD, visible in committee presentations). A clear short-term measure of success is **reward from peers outside IIT** — a publication or appreciation at a conference. Your “peer group” is not the 10 students around you but researchers elsewhere in the world, which is why work is published in **international channels** ; aspire to the **best channels** . 

###### **11.6 Careers after MS / PhD** 

- PhD placement is **nothing like undergraduate placement** ; IIT has mechanisms for industries needing specific skills to reach candidates with those skills. 

- A fraction of graduates go to industry positions that build on their acquired skills; roughly **a quarter** go to positions **not directly related** to their skills (but their background lets them grow into the role). 

- The **biggest employer in India is academia** : about **16 IITs** (more being added), many NITs, state institutions and numerous private institutions — all looking for good faculty with reasonably well-paying positions. 

- Reason to consider academia: the number of young people entering colleges in the next **10–15 years** is huge (demographics); PhD scholars from IIT-type institutions must solve the **training problem** . This is described as a way of being of service to the country. 

###### **11.7 Interaction with the advisor** 

Meet **frequently at first** (to get to know each other); each advisor–student pair later settles into its own comfort zone. Several faculty hold **regular weekly meetings of the whole group** plus more frequent individual meetings; some, once confident in a scholar, allow independent research and meet even less often than weekly. Frequency is decided jointly. An external (part-time) programme has less frequent but equally productive meetings. 

**Conflicts** (student–student or advisor–student) can arise; **professional mechanisms exist** to resolve them at all levels. Only a **minuscule fraction** of the **~2000 PhD students** on campus meet such issues — so do not be fearful about selecting a guide. 

###### **11.8 Advice to aspiring students** 

- Come with an **open mind** and be ready for **interdisciplinary** work — problems cannot be placed in “vertical silos”. 

- Research is a **walk through uncharted territory** ; enjoy the ride. 

- You have succeeded when **peers outside IIT** reward your work; aim for publication in the best international channels. 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — APPLIED MECHANICS** 

- Applied Mechanics = **3 groups** : Fluid, Solid, Bio-mechanics & Bio-medical. 

- Faculty from **8** different undergraduate backgrounds. 

— 25 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- Newer areas enabled by **fast computation** + **advanced analysis/visualization tools** . 

- Success marker: recognition by **peers outside IIT** (publications in international channels). 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Classroom: given 3 of 4 variables find the 4th; research: given **1** of 4 and justify assumptions for the other 3. 

- About **a quarter** of graduates take roles not directly related to their specific skills. 

- Biggest employer of PhDs in India = **academia** . 

- Advisor meetings: **frequent initially** , then reduce — not a fixed rule; decided between advisor and student. 

- Success = recognition by an **outside** peer group, not the immediate lab-mates. 

— 26 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 12 · Research in Chemical Engineering** 

_Covers: Lecture 36 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **12.1 Traditional areas** 

Chemical engineering is one of the old fields. Traditional research is built on **fluid flow, heat transfer and mass transfer** , applied especially to **multiphase systems** — e.g., a **bubble column** (gas bubbles sparged through a liquid column) — together with **residence-time distribution** studies. There is a large body of existing literature in these areas. 

###### **12.2 Old areas with a new emphasis** 

###### **Traditional vs newer emphasis** 

|**Traditional area**|**Newer application / depth**|
|---|---|
|Multiphase fluid flow|Fuel cells (**flooding**occurs in fuel cells as well as in absorption columns),<br>**microfluidics**(flow in micro channels, possibly multiphase)|
|Heat transfer (pool boiling, flow<br>boiling)|**Microwave heating**, e.g., for large-scale sterilization|
|Experimental measurement|From gross measurements (overall**pressure drop**) to local<br>measurements such as**PIV**|
|Modelling|From reactor-level modelling →**CFD**→**lattice Boltzmann**modelling|
|Systems and control|Evolved to**process systems engineering**— whole flow-sheet design,<br>system identification, data analysis; borrows signal-processing ideas from<br>electrical engineering; applied to fuel cells, control of microfluidic devices|



**Process intensification** — integrate 2 or 3 processes into **one operation** (for example, carrying out **reaction and separation simultaneously** ), including **miniaturization of equipment** . Result: much less energy consumption and lower environmental impact. There is no hard line between traditional and novel; one topic can have a traditional feel and another a newer feel. 

###### **12.3 New and emerging areas** 

- **Conducting polymers** for devices (polymers were traditionally used for insulation). 

- **Colloidal systems** designed for photonic or drug-carrier applications. 

- **Drop drying** (a simple fluid-mechanical technique) used to diagnose materials. 

- Novel **electrode materials** prepared using nanomaterials (smart devices). 

- **Bio-processing** — using **microbes to prepare nanoparticles** . 

- Processing/optimising materials, e.g., for **semiconductor** applications (important for the last ~2 decades); large-scale **food processing** (e.g., microwave thawing). 

- **Molecular-scale simulation** — chemical engineers deal with atoms, molecules and chemical transformations. 

###### **12.4 Industry relevance** 

The Government of India has identified about **25 key sectors** under “Make in India”; chemical engineering is centrally involved in about **13–15** of them. Examples: **oil and gas, pharmaceuticals, the chemical sector, biotechnology, wellness** (research linked to traditional medicines) — through raw-material processing, separations and process engineering to obtain a material of desired quality. Also **avionics and electronic systems** : sensors (e.g., environmental sensors; artificial muscle that is both sensor and actuator). Energy-related work: gasification; micro-reactors for pollution-treatment devices; CFD studies applied to equipment. 

— 27 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **12.5 Measuring success** 

1. A **clear understanding** of what has been done (bare minimum). 

2. Ability to **explain** it at different levels — to friends and to professionals in the field. 

3. Knowing the **scope and limitations** (research is never complete) — this sets goals for future research. 

4. Ability to identify **new research topics** and to **work independently** (take up students in an academic career). 

5. Publications — a fair representation of the work in the **open literature** (peer-reviewed, accepted by the peer group). 

6. A sign of success: the scholar **knows more than the guide** by the end. 

###### **12.6 Careers after MS / PhD** 

###### **Career routes** 

|**Route**|**Notes**|
|---|---|
|Further research|MS → PhD; PhD → post-doctoral work|
|Academic positions|Teaching/research positions (MS can also join teaching)|
|Industrial R&D|Around 2005, many joined industrial R&D (expansion in India); in recent|
||years more academic positions; the mix changes with time, topic and group|
|Entrepreneurship|A few explore setting up their own venture|



###### **12.7 Issues faced by incoming students** 

Coursework is **rigorous** (fewer courses, but very detailed analysis, assignments and attendance). Expect **computational exposure** — MATLAB, CFD tools, simulators — as an integral part of courses. Lab work must now emphasise **preciseness, accuracy and repeatability** , which undergraduate labs rarely stress. Two mind-set corrections: 

- **Do not anchor research to a UG course** (“I want to do heat transfer”). Research areas related to heat transfer may need very different fields. If it is “in the book”, it is not research. 

- **Do not be swayed by buzzwords** (e.g., “nanotechnology”, assumed to be groundbreaking by definition). Examine topic details; you must learn techniques, understand, generate new results, analyse and communicate. Be **holistic** , not pigeon-holed. 

###### **12.8 Interaction with advisor and peers** 

- Meetings are **frequent in the first year** and reduce gradually as the scholar becomes more independent; usually a **weekly formal meeting** , with additional meetings whenever required. 

- Weekly is very good at the beginning; it may become **once in 2 weeks** if progress is good. 

- Meet the guide when **results are not coming for a prolonged period** — the observation may be wrong, or may be something interesting; do not stay “in a shell”. 

- Talk to **peers** too — meet a colleague **at least monthly** as a sounding board; discuss major initiatives with 2 other students before meeting the advisor. 

###### **12.9 Advice for aspiring students** 

- The **selection process** (short-listing → written test → interview) tests **fundamentals** (fluid mechanics, mass transfer etc.) — be strong in fundamentals, but also browse the department website to see the research areas. 

- Chemical engineering is one of the few engineering areas where **molecules remain at the centre** : from molecular scale to engineering scale, wherever molecular transformations occur, chemical 

— 28 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### engineering is involved — hence boundless R&D scope. 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — CHEMICAL ENGINEERING** 

- Traditional: **fluid flow, heat transfer, mass transfer** in multiphase systems; **bubble column** = gas sparged in liquid. 

- **Process intensification** = integrating 2–3 operations into one (e.g., reaction + separation), miniaturization. 

- **Make in India** : ~25 sectors; chemical engineering central in ~13–15. 

- Selection: short-list → written test → interview, all testing **fundamentals** . 

- Career mix: further research, academic, industrial R&D, entrepreneurship. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- New emphasis ≠ new principles: modern work still rests on **fundamentals** of fluid mechanics, mass transfer etc. 

- UG labs do not stress precision/repeatability; research does. 

- Conducting polymers: polymers were **traditionally insulators** . 

- Flooding occurs in **both** absorption columns **and** fuel cells. 

- Advisor meeting: weekly initially → possibly fortnightly later; meet a peer **at least monthly** . 

- Do not tie research topics to a single UG course or to buzzwords like nanotechnology. 

— 29 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 13 · Research in Civil Engineering** 

_Covers: Lecture 37 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **13.1 Major areas of civil engineering research** 

Civil engineering is often taken for granted, but active research goes on in each of its areas: 

###### **Areas of civil engineering** 

|**Area**|**What it deals with**|
|---|---|
|Structural engineering|Construction of facilities such as buildings and bridges|
|Geotechnical engineering|Foundations; the mechanics of soils; how safe the foundation should be|
|Environmental engineering|How people and constructed facilities affect and interact with the<br>environment|
|Water resources (hydraulic)<br>engineering|Canals, dams, water supply, sewage and sanitary networks|
|Transportation engineering|Making traffic flow better, viability of transport systems, future planning|
|Construction & project management|Making projects more efficient, safer, cost-effective and delivered on<br>time|
|Materials|Concrete and other construction materials|
|Building physics|Comfort of buildings: ventilation, thermal comfort, lighting|



###### **13.2 Newer developments** 

- **New materials:** traditional materials were **stone, brick, cement and mortar** ; now **polymers** are used extensively. 

- **Information technology** affects modelling and prediction — from **traffic flow** to **structural behaviour** and **environmental interactions** on a structure. 

- **Environmental engineering has boomed** (earlier abroad, in India mainly in the last decade): safer water supply; treatment and safe disposal of waste. 

###### **13.3 Problems faced by incoming research students** 

Students generally do not face major problems, but a common issue is the way they studied earlier: **learning answers to specific questions** and being examined on those. They struggle to **analyse new situations** , and expect research to be a series of steps dictated by the guide, but when asked to **analyse results** they fall short. The better part of the **first year** is spent bringing students to the required level. 

###### **13.4 Measuring success** 

- A good researcher can **analyse and reach decisions based on results** . 

- The real test comes after graduation: ideally within **5–10 years** the person should be one of the **top researchers in India** . 

###### **13.5 Careers** 

— 30 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

###### **After the degree** 

|**Degree**|**Typical destination**|
|---|---|
|PhD|**~90 % or more**go into**teaching**; some go into administration —**IAS, IES, IFS**(Indian<br>Forest Service); civil engineering gives a holistic view useful in administration|
|MS / M.Tech|Many go into**industry**— from chemical manufacturers to structural designers; the<br>whole range of the field|



###### **13.6 Interaction with the guide** 

Meet “as often as necessary”. As a **thumb rule, once a week** is good enough. Daily visits to ask “what should I do?” show lack of independence (and the guide should not encourage them). More frequent meetings are good **towards the end of the thesis** when it has to be corrected. Interaction can be **one-to-one or in a group** — a **weekly whole-group research meeting** is typical. 

###### **13.7 Advice** 

- Ask: “ **How is your thesis going to make the world a better place?** ” (a question put in comprehensive exams or research proposals). Believe in yourself, in the problem and in the solution, and know what you are going to solve. 

- Pick a problem that will have some **use or effect** ; understand it and work towards it — this makes research more pleasant and holistic. 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — CIVIL ENGINEERING** 

- Areas: **structural, geotechnical, environmental, water resources, transportation, construction management, materials, building physics** . 

- New: **polymers** , **IT-based modelling** , **environmental engineering** . 

- **~90 %+** of PhDs go into teaching; some to **IAS / IES / IFS** ; MS/M.Tech mostly to industry. 

- Meeting thumb rule: **once a week** . 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Geotechnical = foundations & soil; Water resources = canals, dams, supply, sewage; Environmental = interaction of people/facilities with environment. 

- Typical student weakness: **cannot analyse new situations** (learnt fixed answers to fixed questions). 

- Success test: ability to **analyse and decide from results** . 

- Meeting once a week is a **thumb rule** ; **not daily** ; more often near thesis correction. 

- The signature question: how does the thesis make the world a better place? 

— 31 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 14 · Research in Computer Science and Engineering** 

_Covers: Lecture 38 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **14.1 Traditional areas** 

Four major research areas traditionally (and still today): **hardware, software, applications and theory** . 

###### **Traditional areas** 

|**Area**|**Research concern**|
|---|---|
|Hardware|Make hardware that works and can be sold; concerns:**power consumption,**<br>**weight, heat dissipation**, deployment environment|
|Software — system software|Operating system, compiler, build environments / design kits;**software**<br>**engineering**= tools to build large-scale software|
|Software — applications|Applications suited to different environments|
|Theory (theoretical computer<br>science)|The foundation governing the others: what can be expected when solving a<br>problem and**how much time**execution will take; gives insight into how<br>good a final solution can be before implementing it|



###### **14.2 New areas — everything flows from the Internet of Things (IoT)** 

As computing devices enter every aspect of life (health, schedules, monitoring), new problems arise. The IoT pipeline and the research it opens: 

###### **The IoT chain** 

|**Stage**|**Challenge / research area**|
|---|---|
|1. Sensing|Interdisciplinary: work with sensor makers; e.g., structural health of a building → civil<br>engineers; smart hybrid vehicle → IC-engine experts. Capturing data effectively,<br>efficiently, accurately|
|2. Local processing|Falls under hardware design; must process data with very**limited compute**(cannot<br>put a server at every traffic signal) — small, possibly solar-powered devices|
|3. Communication|Send data to a server with**limited bandwidth**or even no network connectivity (e.g.,<br>remote high-altitude sensing)|
|4. Data science /<br>analytics|“Problem of plenty”;**real-time**requirement (a traffic jam must be reported in<br>seconds, not an hour later)|
|5. Machine learning|Engines learn patterns over a period (a**training phase**) and match new patterns to<br>earlier ones|
|6. Information security|e.g., a remotely monitored**pacemaker**— a hacker could harm the patient. Security is<br>like a habit that must be built early; after ~50–60 years of software it must be<br>**retrofitted**. Nobody can easily certify a system (e.g., a mobile phone) as secure|



###### **14.3 High-performance computing, multi-core and parallelization** 

Processor **clock frequency** has stalled at about **3 – 3.5 GHz** because **power is directly proportional to frequency** ; pushing it higher would literally burn the processor. So the industry moved from a single core to **multiple cores** : instead of 10 GHz you get, e.g., **4 cores at 2.5 GHz** . Today no CPU has just one core. The new research question is how to make all cores work together. 

###### **WORKED EXAMPLE / APPLICATION** 

— 32 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- **Sequential program:** add 100 numbers one after another. 

- **Parallel program:** split into **4 chunks of 25 numbers** ; each of 4 processors adds its 25; then collect the partial results into one answer. 

- **Parallelization** = converting a sequential program into concurrent pieces that run at the same time. Very few people worldwide have this skill, and few universities offer parallel architecture and parallel algorithm courses. 

###### **14.4 Where industry is interested** 

- **Low-power, high-performance hardware** and architectures for different environments (even a network appliance that routes packets is a computer); small devices used in millions for IoT. 

- New **models of computation** : **approximate computing, stochastic computing, non-von-Neumann computing** . 

- **Modelling and verification** of increasingly complex systems (a CPU taught over a whole semester in 1985 is now covered in the first week). 

- **Operating-system kernel** and **compiler optimization** — very few people can modify a kernel (well under 1 % of CS graduates); great potential for core research jobs. 

- **Data science** — “the elephant”: every one of the institute’s 16 departments has data-science problems; a data science laboratory has about 22–27 faculty across departments. 

- The other extreme: **IoT devices with very small memory** → need very efficient coding techniques; and **parallel programming** . 

###### **14.5 Measuring success — “cross-layer” research** 

Earlier, one could be “only a hardware engineer”. Today everything is **cross-layer** . The computing **stack** has **5 layers** : 

###### **Digital circuit → Micro-architecture → Operating system → System software → Application** 

Example: to control temperature, one needs a thermal-aware compiler, operating system, micro-architecture and digital circuit. Success = demonstrating an enhancement in some layer **and** showing the **synergy** across all five layers. Further, a design must be **feasible** : it must consider **cost, performance and marketability** — a brilliant architecture that costs a billion dollars to build is not engineering. 

###### **14.6 Careers and the importance of the foundation** 

Computer science is very broad; MS/PhD specialisation does not trap a person — e.g., theory-trained researchers move into processor verification, VLSI and IT consulting, and chemical graduates have become top software engineers. The key is a strong foundation in the core courses: 

- **Data structures, algorithms, programming languages, computer architecture, operating systems, automata theory** — the “language of computer science”. 

- Use standard textbooks, NPTEL lectures, and **work out the end-of-chapter exercise problems** before teaching or researching. 

###### **14.7 Difficulty for incoming students and the remedy** 

Students often lack the **language of computing — formalism** . If you do not know the language you cannot think in it; ideas then come out in a zig-zag, unorganised way (like narrating from LKG straight to Plus-Two in random order). The remedy: learn the language through: 

- **Discrete Mathematics** (standard books and NPTEL lectures). 

- **Concrete Mathematics** by **Donald Knuth** . 

— 33 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

- Solve at least **20 %** of the problems in a standard book — this teaches how to write proofs, choose proof techniques and organise ideas clearly. 

- Communication skill means conveying concepts and proofs **clearly** , not perfect English. 

###### **14.8 Interaction with guide and peers** 

- **Peer group matters more than the guide.** Large labs (around 100–120 people) work as teams; senior PhD students effectively “micro-manage” juniors while the faculty “macro-manage”. 

- Meet the guide **at least once in a fortnight** , and send **daily or weekly reports** even if little was done. 

- **Observation** is important: e.g., watching traffic at a busy road signal for 2–3 days teaches coordination, clocking, signals and delays — the core ideas of **distributed computing** . 

- Computer science is a **user-driven** field built on others’ problems — interact with other teams and disciplines. 

###### **14.9 Advice** 

- **Work very hard** — competition is intense; at least ~10 groups worldwide often work on the same problem, and missing one conference deadline can lose a year’s work. 

- **Teamwork** from day one and be **ethical** ; single-author papers are largely gone — seminal papers usually have 4–5 authors. 

- Keep the **formal theory** (discrete and concrete mathematics) clear. 

- A problem not solved in ~ **3 years** may become **obsolete** — so PhD problems should not drag beyond about 3 years; tools given to you may already be ~5 years old. 

- Summary formula: **hard work + team spirit + good foundation** . 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — COMPUTER SCIENCE & ENGINEERING** 

- **4** traditional areas: hardware, software, applications, theory. 

- IoT drives: sensing → local processing → communication → data science → machine learning → security. 

- Clock frequency limit ≈ **3–3.5 GHz** since power ∝ frequency → **multi-core** → **parallelization** . 

- Computing stack has **5 layers** ; modern success is **cross-layer** . 

- Foundation: **Discrete Mathematics** + **Concrete Mathematics** (Knuth). 

- Guide meeting: **at least once a fortnight** ; peers matter more. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Power is **directly proportional** to clock frequency → cannot keep raising frequency → multi-core. 

- Sequential vs parallel: the same job split into concurrent pieces (100 numbers → 4 × 25). 

- System software = **OS, compiler, build environments** ; applications are separate. 

- Theory tells **what to expect and how long** a solution will take before implementing it. 

- Success today needs knowledge of the **entire stack** , plus cost/performance/feasibility. 

- Advised textbook route: **Discrete Mathematics** and **Concrete Mathematics** ; attempt ≥ 20 % of exercise problems. 

- A PhD problem can become obsolete in ~ **3 years** . 

— 34 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 15 · Research in Engineering Design** 

_Covers: Lecture 39 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **15.1 A unique department focused on the design process** 

Engineering Design departments are **not common** in the country. In conventional departments (mechanical, electrical, civil …) the undergraduate emphasis is on **analysis** — students analyse something already designed; the **design aspect is not emphasised as much** . This department focuses on the **design process** . Its areas have traditionally centred on **materials** and **controls** for **automotive** and **biomedical** applications. 

###### **15.2 Cutting-edge direction** 

- To be at the forefront, one must **cut across disciplines** : a mechanical engineer must understand the language and constraints of an electrical engineer and vice versa. 

- Entering students are usually strong in one area; this is addressed by **coursework across many disciplines** early on (adds value over a lifetime), instead of staying in a narrow speciality. 

###### **15.3 Industry interest — science vs incremental tweaks** 

###### **Comparison: industry vs academic approach** 

||**Industry-only approach**|**Academic (science-based) approach**|
|---|---|---|
|Typical result|Small tweaks;**incremental**advances;<br>short term|Large jumps; possibly**completely new**<br>**technology**; longer term|
|Basis|Existing practice|**Application of fundamental science**|
|Role|—|Provide the scientific basis for industrially<br>relevant problems with a longer-term view<br>(should hold for all engineering departments)|



Examples of industry collaboration: **automotive** — new **tyre designs** , vehicle **handling** , **stability** , **noise and vibration** ; and **biomedical** industry. Industry also expects close interaction with such a department and problems approached from the industry’s perspective. 

###### **15.4 Measuring success: learn the process, not just the problem** 

Focus on the **process of solving a problem** , not only the particular problem. Real problems are **vaguely stated** — by industry customers and in the early stage of research. The process: 

1. Take a **vaguely stated problem** and make it **more specific** . 

2. Make **specific assumptions** . 

3. Develop or select **appropriate tools** to solve it. 

4. **Defend** the assumptions and the conclusions **coherently** . 

This process carries over to any other discipline. A graduate is successful if he or she has **learned the process** . 

###### **15.5 Careers** 

The department is relatively young (fewer graduates so far). Graduates so far have gone mostly into **industry** — **research labs of biomedical companies** and **automotive R&D** — with a growing trend toward academic careers. 

###### **15.6 Interaction with the guide** 

**Early stage:** frequent meetings — **once a week or even shorter intervals** — because turning a vague problem into a tractable one is **one of the most difficult parts** of research methodology. 

— 35 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

**Later:** once the stage is learning and implementing tools, the scholar works independently and meetings can be **as little as once a month** — to avoid “spoon-feeding” of tools. By the end of research training one should be able to learn tools from an undergraduate or even a mathematics-level textbook independently. 

###### **15.7 Advice** 

- Develop broad **interdisciplinary interests** and curiosity about subjects not directly related to your speciality — useful in industry and in academia. 

- Keep the **big picture** : each person contributes a small part of solving a societal need; knowing where your solution fits makes your contribution larger. 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — ENGINEERING DESIGN** 

- Focus: **design process** (not just analysis); areas: materials and controls for automotive and biomedical. 

- Industry → **incremental** tweaks; science → **disruptive** jumps. 

- Success = learning the **process** : vague problem → specific → assumptions → tools → defend. 

- Meetings: weekly (or less) early on; **about monthly** later. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Conventional UG curricula emphasise **analysis** ; Engineering Design emphasises **design** . 

- Early research phase needs **more** frequent meetings; tool-learning phase needs **fewer** (even monthly). 

- Success is measured by whether the scholar learned the **process** , not the specific problem. 

- Graduates so far: mostly **industry R&D** (biomedical labs, automotive). 

- Real-world problems are **vaguely stated** ; a key skill is making them specific. 

— 36 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

##### **Chapter 16 · Research in Humanities and Social Sciences** 

_Covers: Lecture 40 — department module (IIT Madras)_ 

###### **16.1 Nature of the department and traditional areas** 

A Humanities and Social Sciences department in an IIT is characterised by its **intra-disciplinary and multidisciplinary nature** . 

###### **Research areas** 

|**Category**|**Areas**|
|---|---|
|Traditional|Economics, literature, linguistics, philosophy, history, sociology|
|Added more recently|**Developmental studies**;**China studies**;**European studies**(European Union);<br>**Indo-German Centre for Sustainability**studies|



###### **16.2 Emerging areas within disciplines** 

- **History:** history of science. 

- **Developmental studies:** intersections with politics, gender, sociology of religion. 

- **Linguistics:** language and technology; use of technology in teaching methods. 

- **Literature** sub-divisions: American, African, English, Indian literature and Indian writing in English. 

- Most incoming students already have a specific area in mind (e.g., African or American literature), not just “English”. 

###### **16.3 Boundaries of humanities** 

**There is no limit.** Research ranges over **health, policy, technology, ethics, religion** and more. Being located inside a technological institution **expands** possibilities: data and collaborators are readily available — e.g., technology in health (from computer science, biotechnology, electrical engineering) leads to social-science questions about its impact, assessment and ethical practice. 

###### **16.4 Challenges for students** 

- The department is not a department of a single discipline, so each discipline has a **limited number of faculty** ; students must be prepared to work with few specialists. 

- Linguistics, for example, has **4 core areas** ; one person has limited expertise in all four (general linguistics). Specialists in sub-areas (e.g., phonology, computational linguistics, morphology) may have to be consulted from outside. 

- Strength: a **“virtual discipline”** — e.g., 4–5 colleagues working on linguistics in 4 different departments; a local pool of specialists in emerging areas. 

- Resource problems of the past are reduced because materials are now available **electronically** . 

###### **Four core areas of linguistics (as listed)** 

|**Core area of linguistics**|**Deals with**|
|---|---|
|Syntax|Sentences|
|Morphology|Words|
|Phonology|Sounds|
|Sociolinguistics|Language and society|



###### **16.5 Value for industry and society** 

— 37 — 

NPTEL · Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) · Study Guide, Lectures 31–40 

What is learned in humanities and social sciences is most useful **outside** the boundaries of an academic institution, whereas engineering expertise is demonstrated mostly among peers of the same discipline. Industries need a **cordial relationship with the local community** . Given India’s **multiculturalism and multilingualism** , they must understand local languages, politics and cultural practices — a role for social scientists. Applying humanities research can reduce socio-political conflicts around industrial projects. Graduates are also being hired in **marketing** and other roles needing human interaction (including start-ups). 

###### **16.6 Meeting the supervisor** 

In language research the **whole world is the laboratory** (“the laboratory for research on language begins where all laboratories end”). Though some areas need controlled environments, in general: **one hour of meaningful, content-rich discussion per week** is sufficient and required — evaluating what has been done, what is next and where things stand. The scholar should come to the next meeting **prepared with at least 75 %** of what was discussed in the previous meeting. 

###### **16.7 Careers** 

- Most master’s students go on to **PhD or higher studies** (the department has a **5-year integrated programme** after Class 12; students are encouraged to move elsewhere for higher studies to test what they learned). 

- Others join **government civil services** or **non-government organizations** (gender equality, sustainability, water-resource management, health-care management). 

- Unconventional openings are growing — e.g., **marketing** . 

- PhD graduates: **post-doctoral programmes** and **teaching/research** positions. 

###### **16.8 Advice** 

1. Be **committed and disciplined** to your academic discipline. 

2. Research is a **full-time activity** (“24 hours”); it should reflect your interest and not be a burden — if it becomes a burden, interest is being lost. 

3. **Read beyond your own discipline** — it builds the habit of reading, gives holistic development and improves work in your own discipline. 

###### **QUICK-REVISION SHEET — HUMANITIES & SOCIAL SCIENCES** 

- Traditional areas: **economics, literature, linguistics, philosophy, history, sociology** ; plus developmental, China, European and Indo-German sustainability studies. 

- **No boundary** on topics: health, policy, technology, ethics, religion. 

- Linguistics core areas: **syntax, morphology, phonology, sociolinguistics** . 

- Meeting: **1 hour/week** ; prepare **≥ 75 %** of the previous discussion. 

- Career routes: higher studies, civil services, NGOs, marketing, post-doc, teaching. 

###### **⚠ NPTEL EXAM TRAPS — SPECIAL CONDITIONS & EXCEPTIONS** 

- Language research: **the whole world is the laboratory** . 

- Humanities graduates’ skills are valued mostly **outside** academic boundaries (industry–community relations, multicultural/multilingual contexts). 

- Weekly meeting = **1 hour** ; scholar prepared with **75 %** of the earlier discussion. 

- Disadvantage of IIT placement: few faculty per discipline; advantage: easy access to technological domains and data. 

- Three-point advice: discipline, full-time commitment + enjoyment, reading beyond one’s discipline.

|**Topic**|**Formula / fact**|**Notes**|
|---|---|---|
|Sample mean|x̄ = Σxi/n|estimator of μ|
|Sample variance|s² = Σ(xi− x̄)²/(n − 1)|df = n − 1|
|Mean of x̄|E(x̄) = μ||
|Variance of x̄|V(x̄) = σ²/n|smaller than σ²|
|CLT|x̄ ≈ Normal(μ, σ²/n) for n > 30|non-normal parent|
|Confidence interval (σ<br>known)|x̄ ± zα/2·σ/√n|100(1 − α)%; α = 0.05 → 95 %|
|Standard normal|Z = (x − μ)/σ|mean 0, SD 1|
|t variable|t = (x̄ − μ)/(s/√n)|n − 1 df; small n, σ unknown|
|Chi-square|χ² = (n − 1)s²/σ²|n − 1 df; P(χ² > χ²α,k) = α|
|F variable|F = (S1²/σ1²)/(S2²/σ2²)|two df; f0.05,10,10= 2.98|

|Two-factor ANOVA|df: a − 1, b − 1, (a − 1)(b − 1)|F = MS/MSEfor each source|
|2<sup>n</sup>factorial|runs = 2<sup>n</sup>|3 factors → 8 runs|
|Multiple regression|y = β0+ Σβjxj+ ε|p = k + 1; n > k|
|Matrix model|Y = Xβ + ε|X has a column of ones|
|Least-squares estimate|β̂ = (X′X)<sup>−1</sup>X′Y||
|Error SS|SSE= Y′Y − β̂′X′Y|SST− SSR|
|Regression ANOVA df|k, n − k − 1, n − 1|MSR= SSR/k|
|Adjusted R²|1 − [SSE/(n − p)]/[SST/(n − 1)]|penalises extra parameters|
|Second-order model|y = β0+ Σβixi+ Σβijxixj+ Σβiixi² + ε|2 factors → 6 parameters|
|CCD (k = 2)|4 factorial + 2k = 4 axial + centre repeats|centre repeats 4–5|
|Stationary point|xs= −½B<sup>−1</sup>b|from b + 2Bx = 0|
|Nature of optimum|eigenvalues of B: all + → min; all − → max||

##### **Chapter 19 · Rapid-Fire Fact Sheet (Read Before the Exam)** 

###### **A. Statistics and Design of Experiments** 

- Hypothesis tests concern the **population** ; the sample is only evidence. 

- Rejecting H0 means automatic acceptance of H1; otherwise write “do not reject H0”. 

- **Type I** = reject a true H0 (more serious). **Type II** = do not reject a false H0 (less serious). 

- The p-value is linked with the probability of a Type I error; large F → small p. 

- Sample variance divides by **n − 1** because deviations from the mean sum to zero. 

- E(x̄) = μ; V(x̄) = σ²/n. Larger n → narrower sampling distribution. 

- CLT: large samples (> 30) → sample mean approximately normal. 

- α = 0.05 ⇒ 95 % CI ⇒ each tail 0.025. 

- A CI is on the **population mean** ; 95 out of 100 intervals are expected to bracket μ. 

- Broad interval = more confidence, less precision; narrow = more precision, less confidence. 

- t: small n, σ unknown, df = n − 1. χ² = (n − 1)s²/σ², positive only. F = ratio of variances, two df. 

- F distribution (not χ²) is directly used in DoE; f0.05,10,10 = 2.98. 

- “Treatment” = level of a factor. Error variability comes from repeats. 

- One-factor ANOVA df: **a − 1** (treatment), **a(n − 1)** (error). Two-factor interaction df: **(a − 1)(b − 1)** . 

- Randomize run order so that uncontrollable effects (e.g., heating of the photocopier) spread evenly. 

- 2<sup>3</sup> factorial = 8 runs. Factorial designs reveal interactions; one-factor-at-a-time does not. 

- Parallel interaction lines = no interaction. Cricket: tea + heavy bat 24; beer + heavy bat 51. 

- Regression: k regressors → k + 1 parameters; n > k; empirical; do not extrapolate. 

- β̂ = (X′X)<sup>−1</sup> X′Y. Regression ANOVA df: k and n − k − 1; total n − 1. 

- R² = 1 when parameters = runs; check adjusted R²; if it falls on adding terms, do not add them. 

- CCD for k = 2: 4 + 4 + centre (4–5 repeats). Centre points detect curvature (β11 + β22); axial points give individual quadratic terms but not interactions. 

- RSM: screening experiments first; steepest ascent; xs = −½B<sup>−1</sup> b; eigenvalues all positive → minimum, all negative → maximum. 

###### **B. Department modules** 

- **Applied Mechanics:** 3 groups (fluid, solid, bio); faculty from 8 UG backgrounds; PG-only department; enablers: fast computation + analysis/visualization tools; ~¼ of graduates in unrelated roles; academia biggest employer; ~16 IITs; ~2000 PhD students on campus. 

- **Chemical Engineering:** bubble column; process intensification (reaction + separation together); PIV; lattice Boltzmann; Make in India ~25 sectors, 13–15 with chemical engineering central; selection = short-list, written test, interview on fundamentals; meet a peer at least monthly. 

- **Civil Engineering:** structural, geotechnical, environmental, water resources, transportation, construction management, materials, building physics; polymers as new materials; ~90 %+ PhDs teach; IAS / IES / IFS; weekly meeting thumb rule. 

- **Computer Science:** hardware, software, applications, theory; IoT chain; frequency limit 3–3.5 GHz; multi-core; parallelization; 5-layer stack; Discrete + Concrete Mathematics (Knuth); ≥ 20 % of problems; guide at least once in a fortnight; PhD problems obsolete in ~3 years. 

- **Engineering Design:** design process vs analysis; materials and controls for automotive/biomedical; industry = incremental, science = disruptive; process: vague → specific → assumptions → tools → defend; meetings weekly early, monthly later. 

- **Humanities & Social Sciences:** intra-/multidisciplinary; no boundaries; linguistics: syntax, morphology, phonology, sociolinguistics; 5-year integrated programme; 1 hour per week; 75 % prepared; discipline, full-time commitment, reading beyond discipline.

NPTEL CERTIFICATION MASTER STUDY GUIDE • PART 1 OF 2 (LECTURES 41–45) 

##### Introduction to Research (Research Methodology) 

Domain-Specific Research Methodologies: Mechanical, Metallurgical & Materials, Ocean, Management, and Aerospace 

MODULE 1: RESEARCH IN MECHANICAL CORE ENGINEERING ENGINEERING (LECTURE 41) BRANCH 

###### 1.A CLEAR CONCEPT EXPLANATION: ARCHITECTURE OF MECHANICAL ENGINEERING RESEARCH 

Mechanical Engineering, alongside Civil Engineering and Electrical Engineering, constitutes one of the three oldest branches of engineering. Rather than existing as isolated silos, traditional research in Mechanical Engineering is organized into three core verticals that operate sequentially to form an integrated End-to-End Design Workflow. When developing complex mechanical hardware—such as an internal combustion engine, a jet propulsion engine, or a refrigerator compressor—researchers progress systematically from theoretical energy laws to physical fabrication. 

1. Vertical 1 — Thermal Engineering: Focuses on the thermodynamic aspects of engineering systems, including energy conversion, energy conservation, and energy waste analysis. In this foundational stage, theoretical requirements are calculated and sized (e.g., establishing the exact target pressure and temperature required inside an engine cylinder or compressor). 

2. Vertical 2 — Design of Components: Once thermodynamic targets are established on paper, the Design vertical addresses the material and structural aspects of the equipment. Researchers determine how to physically construct devices capable of withstanding the required thermodynamic loads by selecting appropriate materials to fabricate components such as pistons and piston rods. 

3. Vertical 3 — Manufacturing: Logically occurs last after both thermodynamic and material designs are frozen on paper. It investigates the physical realization of components through special tools, special fixtures, specialized machinery, and advanced machining processes. 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
1. THERMAL ENGINEERING 2. COMPONENT DESIGN 3. MANUFACTURING<br>Conceptualization & Energy Analysis Equipment & Material Selection Physical Realization of Frozen Design<br>Sizing P, T & Energy Conversion Withstanding Target P & T Loads Special Tools, Fixtures & Machining<br><!-- End of picture text -->

Figure 1.1: The End-to-End Design progression linking the three traditional verticals of Mechanical Engineering. 

###### MODERN EVOLUTION: DEEP SPECIALIZATION & INTERDISCIPLINARITY 

- Hyper-Specialization within Verticals (Diesel Engine Emissions Case): Modern research inside traditional verticals emphasizes reducing pollution and emissions in internal combustion (Diesel) engines via three specialized fronts: (1) investigating fundamental combustion properties of alternative fuels superior to conventional hydrocarbons; (2) analyzing chemical kinetics of alternative fuel combustion; and (3) utilizing lasers and high-speed cameras to capture real-time optical images inside operating engines to optimize fuel spray and combustion geometry. 

- Interdisciplinary Integration: Modern mechanical systems rely heavily on cross-disciplinary integration. Contemporary automobiles are governed by embedded electronic control systems, driving collaboration between Electrical and Mechanical engineers. Emerging cross-cutting fields include Mechatronics, HighPerformance Computing (HPC), Acoustics, Propulsion, and Nanotechnology (applied in both Thermal Engineering and Manufacturing to achieve ultra-precise surface finishes and machining quality). 

NPTEL Master Study Guide — Introduction to Research (Lectures 41–45)
